Formal category theory in ∞ \infty -equipments II: Lax functors, monoidality and fibrations
Jaco Ruit

Abstract
. We study the framework of ∞ \infty -equipments which is designed to produce well-behaved theories for different generalizations of ∞ \infty -categories in a synthetic and uniform fashion. We consider notions of (lax) functors between these equipments, closed monoidal structures on these equipments, and fibrations internal to these equipments. As a main application, we will demonstrate that the foundations of internal ∞ \infty -category theory can be readily obtained using this formalism.



1.
Introduction

There are many useful generalizations of
\infty
-categories such as enriched, indexed, internal or fibered versions. These variants have tailored notions
of (co)limits, adjunctions, point-wise Kan extensions and more, that (generally) differ from the corresponding notions for their underlying
\infty
-categories.
Consequently, one would need to redevelop the theory for each of the variants. For example, Heine recently worked out a very complete theory of enriched
\infty
-categories
[
Hei24
]
.
Weighted colimits of enriched
\infty
-categories were also studied by Hinich
[
Hin21
]
.
Indexed
\infty
-category theory, also called
parametrized

\infty
-category theory, has been introduced by Barwick, Dotto, Glasman, Nardin and Shah
[
BDG+16
]
and was further studied
by Shah in
[
Sha18
]
and
[
Sha21
]
. This theory has now been subsumed by the theory of
\infty
-categories internal to a general
\infty
-topos
that has been studied
by Rasekh
[
Ras22
]
, and Martini and Wolf
[
Mar21
]
,
[
MW21
]
.

The goal of this work is to (further) develop the
\infty
-categorical framework, introduced in
[
Rui23
]
, that is
designed to produce these theories for the different variants of
\infty
-categories all at once. This is a generalization
of a program set out in the context of (strict) categories
[
SW78
]
,
[
Woo82
]
,
[
Ver92
]
,
[
Shu08
]
. The key role in the
\infty
-categorical formalism
is played by the
\infty
-equipments

[
Rui23
, Subsection 3.2]
. These are certain double
\infty
-categorical structures that support a good notion of
an internal
formal category theory
. This includes notions of weighted (co)limits, point-wise Kan extensions and more, see
[
Rui23
, Section 6]
.
In this sense, every
\infty
-equipment yields
a category theory
for its objects.
A prototypical example of an
\infty
-equipment is given by the double
\infty
-category

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}

of
\infty
-categories, with
functors and profunctors as vertical and
horizontal arrows respectively
[
Rui23
, Section 4]
. The formal category theory associated with this equipment
yields the well-known theory of
\infty
-categories. More generally,
Haugseng
[
Hau16
]
has constructed a candidate
\infty
-equipment of enriched
\infty
-categories. We expect that its formal category theory captures the correct notions
for enriched
\infty
-category theory.

We have also constructed an
\infty
-equipment

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})

of
\infty
-categories internal to an
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
in
[
Rui23
, Section 5]
.
As evidence of the power of the equipmental approach, we will demonstrate in
8
that one can obtain the foundations of internal category theory
by interpreting the formal category theory associated with the equipment
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
. As mentioned earlier, internal category theory was previously studied by Martini and Wolf
[
MW21
]
by abstracting Lurie’s work
[
Lur09
]
so that
it may be interpreted in any
\infty
-topos. For presheaf
\infty
-toposes (the
indexed
case), this has been studied by Shah
[
Sha18
]
before.
Both these approaches are fundamentally different from the one that we present in this article. The equipmental approach
has also been used by Shulman in
[
Shu13
]
where strict equipments appear to study indexed (more generally, enriched indexed) categories.

The main goal of this paper is to expand the toolbox of
\infty
-equipments so that we may express more aspects of category theory inside
\infty
-equipments. This will be necessary to
give the complete foundations of internal category theory later. To this end, we will also need to develop
more aspects of double
\infty
-categorical theory that is not yet present in the current literature.
This work crucially builds upon the material that has been developed in the prequel paper
[
Rui23
]

and material from
[
Rui24
]
. We will
review some parts of this in
2
.

1.1.
Lax functors

In
3
, we extend the notion of (normal) lax functors between double categories of Grandis and Paré
[
GP99
]
to double
\infty
-categories.
Such an extension was recently also considered by Abellán
[
Abe23
]
, albeit using a slightly different set-up than we present here.
We will construct a double
\infty
-category
\mathbb{D}\mathrm{blCat}_{\infty}
of double
\infty
-categories in the process. Its vertical arrows are functors and its horizontal arrows
are lax functors. Much of the theory about lax functors between double categories goes through in the
\infty
-categorical setting. For instance, in
3.4
, we will
generalize Shulman’s result that asserts that lax functors between equipments preserve cartesian cells
[
Shu08
, Proposition 6.8]
, with essentially the same proof.

We also study the notion of a lax adjunction between lax functors. The main instance of such an adjunction (covered in
3.11
) occurs as follows:
every geometric morphism
f:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}
between
\infty
-toposes gives rise to a lax adjunction

f^{*}:\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{F})\rightleftarrows\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}):f_{*}.

Whenever
f
is a geometric embedding, i.e. when the direct image functor
of
f
is fully faithful, then we will see that

f_{*}
is a double
\infty
-categorical analog of an inclusion of a reflective subcategory. We will
develop this notion of a
reflective lax inclusion
independently, as well as a
local
variant, in
4.1
.

Moreover, we introduce a notion of
proper
and
smooth
lax functors between
\infty
-equipments in
4.2
. These are functors
that preserve the left (resp. right) Kan extensions internal to the formal category theories (see
[
Rui23
, Subsection 6.3]
) of the
\infty
-equipments.
Our main examples of these occur as
base change
functors associated to suitable
\infty
-equipments (see
4.3
), and this
gives rise to a notion of proper and smooth arrows in these
\infty
-equipments. This generalizes Lurie’s notions of proper and smooth functors
[
Lur09
, Subsection 4.1.2]
.

1.2.
Monoidal structures

Now that we have access to a notion of lax adjunctions, we can make sense of
monoidal closed
double
\infty
-categories in
5
. In the (strict) double categorical literature, there
are various definitions of monoidal double categories, see for instance the definition of Grandis-Paré
[
GP04
]
or the one of Hansen-Shulman
[
HS19
]
. Our definition will be a generalization of the last one; we define
monoidal double
\infty
-categories to be monoids in the
\infty
-category of double
\infty
-categories. The
closure
condition is then phrased as the existence
of a normal lax right adjoint to the functor that tensors with a fixed object (see
5.9
). This right adjoint supplies the monoidal double
\infty
-category
with a good notion of
internal homs
that have double categorical functoriality.

We may then study the
\infty
-equipments that admit such a closed monoidal structure. There is a relatively easy way of
obtaining such structures by assembling level-wise cartesian monoidal structures, see
5.6
. We will use this to demonstrate that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
admits a (cartesian) closed monoidal structure.
Whenever one has an arrow
w:i\rightarrow j
and an object
x
in a closed monoidal
\infty
-equipment
\mathscr{P}
,
one can ask for the existence of a left adjoint to the restriction arrow

w^{*}:[j,x]\rightarrow[i,x]

in the underlying vertical
(\infty,2)
-category of
\mathscr{P}
. We will see in
5.2
that this problem can be studied
using the point-wise left Kan extensions along
w
in the formal category theory of
\mathscr{P}
(see
[
Rui23
, Definition 6.19]
),
similarly to ordinary (
\infty
-)category theory.

1.3.
Tabulations and fibrations

A large portion of the paper is devoted to developing an abstract notion of
two-sided discrete fibrations
internal to double
\infty
-categories,
and in particular, internal to
\infty
-equipments. This will be the goal of
6
. These fibrations are particular spans in the vertical fragment of a double
\infty
-category that generalize two-sided discrete fibrations between
\infty
-categories (see
6.3
).
Our notion is an adaptation of a 2-categorical development by Street
[
Str74
]

to a double
\infty
-categorical setting, and we will draw parallels between the two approaches in
6.21
.
In our context, the definition of these fibrations is phrased using the double
\infty
-categorical variant of Grandis’ and Pareś notion of
tabulations
or
tabulators

[
GP99
]

of horizontal arrows.
The double
\infty
-categories that admit all tabulations for horizontal arrows are called
tabular
.
In such a double
\infty
-category, every horizontal arrow has an associated two-sided discrete fibration.
The main result of
6
is the verification
that our two-sided discrete fibrations are so ‘representably’ as well:

Theorem A
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a tabular double
\infty
-category.
Let
(p,q):e\rightarrow x\times y
be a two-sided discrete fibration in the vertical
(\infty,2)
-category
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
of
\mathscr{P}
. Then
(p,q)
induces a two-sided discrete
fibration of
\infty
-categories

(p,q)_{*}:\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(z,e)\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(z,x)\times\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(z,y)

for every
z\in\mathscr{P}
.

In the paper, this result will be obtained as a corollary of
6.6
. The converse of
A
is not always true. The two-sided discrete fibrations rely on the horizontal fragments of double
\infty
-categories as well.

In
7
, we continue to study the tabular
\infty
-equipments for which the passage from horizontal arrows
to associated two-sided discrete fibrations is an embedding, in a particular sense.
We first show that one may always construct a
span representation
for a tabular
\infty
-equipment, generalizing work of
Grandis-Paré
[
GP17
]
and Niefield
[
Nie12
]
for strict double categories.

Theorem B
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a tabular double
\infty
-category with pullbacks. Then there exists a lax
functor

\rho:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)})

from
\mathscr{P}
to the double
\infty
-category of spans in the vertical
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}
of
\mathscr{P}
.
This functors acts as the identity on the underlying vertical
\infty
-categories and carries
horizontal arrows to their associated two-sided discrete fibrations.

This theorem appears as
7.1
. Its proof makes use of a universal property of the double
\infty
-category of spans, exhibited by Haugseng in
[
Hau21b
]
.

We may then characterize the tabular
\infty
-equipments
\mathscr{P}
that have the property that the associated span representations

\rho
are
horizontally locally reflective
, in the sense that the induced functors

\rho_{x,y}:\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}/(x\times y)=\mathrm{Hor}(\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}))(x,y)

on horizontal mapping
\infty
-categories are inclusions of reflective subcategories for
x,y\in\mathscr{P}
.
This characterization is also supplied by
7.1
. The
\infty
-equipments that
have this property will be called
fibrational
. We will demonstrate that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
is fibrational for every
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
(see
7.5
).

Using the span representation, we develop the following theory for a fibrational
\infty
-equipments:

•

We show that two-sided discrete fibrations are closed under base change (
7.11
), and identify the
comma fibrations
that classify certain horizontal arrows (
7.12
).

•

We prove a fibrational variant of the Yoneda lemma (
7.13
). In a 2-categorical context, such a result
was proven by Street in
[
Str74
, Corollary 16]
. For
\infty
-cosmoses, there is a similar result proven by Riehl-Verity
[
RV22
, Theorem 5.7.1]
.

•

For a
pointed
(see
[
Rui23
, Definition 6.30]
) and fibrational
\infty
-equipment, we introduce
left
and
right fibrations
(
7.14
).

•

For a pointed and cartesian closed fibrational
\infty
-equipment, we introduce
slice and coslice objects
(
7.16
). We use this to show that certain weighted colimits can be computed
as conical limits (
7.18
). This generalizes a result of Haugseng
[
Hau22
, Theorem 1.2]
for
\infty
-categories. As a corollary, we explain that
left Kan extensions can be computed by conical colimits, similar to the case for ordinary
\infty
-categories (
7.19
).

•

In
7.4
, we characterize the pointed and fibrational
\infty
-equipments that admit
comprehensive factorizations
. In such an equipment, every arrow
may be factored (essentially uniquely) as an initial arrow followed by a left fibration. We show that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
has these factorizations. In such equipments, coconical colimits may be computed
as initial objects in coslices (
7.27
).

The span representations may also be used to obtain a characterization of the double
\infty
-categories of spans of
[
Hau18
]
.
This may be viewed as double
\infty
-categorical version of a result established by Carboni, Kasangian, and Street
[
CKS84
, Theorem 4]
about recognizing span bicategories.

Corollary C
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a double
\infty
-category. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathscr{P}
is equivalent to a double
\infty
-category of spans in an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
with pullbacks,

(2)

\mathscr{P}
is a fibrational
\infty
-equipment and every span in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}
is a two-sided discrete fibration (with respect to
\mathscr{P}
),

(3)

the span representation of
B
is a strict functor and an equivalence.

This appears as
7.8
in the article.

1.4.
Applications to internal category theory

Finally, in
8
, we will give the promised application of the formalism developed in this paper. Here, we
interpret the formal category theory in the
\infty
-equipment

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})

of

\infty
-categories internal to a fixed
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
to recover the fundamentals of internal category theory.
The content and strategy of
8
may be briefly summarized as follows:

•

As a first step, we will characterize the companions and conjoints in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
in
8.8
. To prove this, we will make use of the characterization of
[
Rui24
, Theorem 4.6]

of companionships and conjunctions in functor double
\infty
-categories. This will be crucial to understand the formal category theory.

•

We show that the two-sided discrete fibrations in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
are precisely the ones that are representably so, providing
a converse to
A
in this particular case.

•

We show that left and right fibrations are proper and smooth respectively; this was shown earlier by Martini
[
Mar21
, Proposition 4.4.7]
.

•

We provide an internal variant of Quillen’s theorem A (
8.16
), see also
[
Mar21
, Corollary 4.4.8]
.

•

We show that
colimits weighted by right fibrations
may be computed as conical colimits. In turn, these may be viewed as initial objects in certain internal coslice categories (see
8.14
).
This proves that the notions of colimits in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
coincides with that of Martini-Wolf
[
MW21
]
(
8.15
).

•

We give the existence conditions and computation of left Kan extensions internal to
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
in terms of colimits, essentially as known from classical category theory (see
8.17
). This also appears in
[
Sha18
]
(for a special case).

•

We give existence conditions for left adjoints to internal restriction functors in terms of left Kan extensions (
8.19
). This was shown by Shah
[
Sha18
]
(again, in a special case) and Martini-Wolf
[
MW21
]
as well.

We stress that the relevant double
\infty
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
is all the input structure that we use to develop these aspects.

Conventions

We will use the language of
\infty
-categories as developed by Joyal and Lurie. Moreover, we use the following notational conventions:

•

The
\infty
-categories of spaces (i.e.
\infty
-groupoids) and
\infty
-categories are denoted by
\mathscr{S}
and
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
respectively.

•

For an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
, we write
\textup{PSh}(\mathscr{C}):=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{S})
for the
\infty
-category of presheaves on
\mathscr{C}
.

•

We will use uppercase notation for
(\infty,2)
-categorical upgrades of particular
\infty
-categories. The double
\infty
-categorical variants will be decorated with blackboard bolds.
For instance, in this article, we will use both the
(\infty,2)
-category
\textup{CAT}_{\infty}
and the double
\infty
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
of
\infty
-categories.

•

We will suppress the notation of an arrow, such as ‘
\Rightarrow
’, when denoting 2-cells in double
\infty
-categories. For example, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

denotes a 2-cell in a double
\infty
-category with
F,G
and
f,g
the corresponding source-target horizontal arrows and vertical arrows respectively.

2.
Preliminaries

Our first section is dedicated to reviewing the relevant material in
[
Rui23
]
and
[
Rui24
]
that will be used throughout this article. Moreover,
we take this opportunity to set up notation and introduce conventions.

2.1.
Double
\infty
-categories

In
[
Rui23
]
and
[
Rui24
]
, we have studied the following localizations of the
\infty
-category
\textup{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})
of bisimplicial spaces:

Table 2.1.1.
Relevant notions of double and 2-fold Segal spaces

Reflective subcategory

Local objects

\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})

\subset

\textup{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})

Double Segal spaces

\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}

\subset

\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})

Double
\infty
-categories

\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}^{lc}

\subset

\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}

Locally complete double
\infty
-categories

\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{S})

\subset

\textup{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})

2-Fold Segal spaces

\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{S})^{lc}

\subset

\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{S})

Locally complete 2-fold Segal spaces

\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}

\subset

\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{S})^{lc}

(\infty,2)
-Categories

For the definitions, we refer to the concise summary given in
[
Rui24
, Section 2]
.

Convention 2.1
.

The image of
([n],[m])
under the Yoneda embedding

\Delta^{\times 2}\rightarrow\textup{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})

will be denoted by

[n,m]
. This is a (locally complete) double
\infty
-category. We refer to the objects
[0,0],[1,0],[0,1]
and
[1,1]
as the
free living object (0-cell)
, the
free living horizontal
and
vertical arrow (1-cell)
,
and the
free living 2-cell
, respectively.

Example 2.2
.

In
[
Hau18
]
, Haugseng constructs the double
\infty
-category of spans

\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathscr{C})

in an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
with pullbacks.
Its objects and vertical arrows correspond to object and morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
respectively, and its horizontal arrows are given by spans of morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
.

Notation 2.3
.

There are three relevant involutions on
\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})
:

(1)

the
transpose functor

(-)^{\mathrm{t}}:\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})\rightarrow\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S}),

induced by the involution

\Delta\times\Delta\rightarrow\Delta\times\Delta:([n],[m])\mapsto([m],[n])
,

(2)

the
horizontal opposite functor

(-)^{\mathrm{hop}}:\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})\rightarrow\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})

induced by the involution

\Delta\times\Delta\xrightarrow{\mathrm{op}\times\mathrm{id}}\Delta\times\Delta
,

(3)

the
vertical opposite functor

(-)^{\mathrm{vop}}:\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})\rightarrow\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})

induced by the involution

\Delta\times\Delta\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\times\mathrm{op}}\Delta\times\Delta
.

See also
[
Rui23
, Definition 2.6]
.
Note that (2) and (3) restrict to involutions on
\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}
and
\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}^{lc}
, but
(-)^{\mathrm{t}}
does not.

Convention 2.4
.

One may realize
\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}
as a reflective subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\textup{Cat}_{\infty})
as well.
Namely, a double
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}
can be viewed as a simplicial
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
so that

\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}([m],\mathscr{P}^{\prime}_{n})\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}([n,m],\mathscr{P}),

naturally for
[n],[m]\in\Delta
. This has been discussed in
[
Rui23
, Section 2.1]
. We will use
this perspective on many
occasions throughout this article, and the described inclusion

\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\textup{Cat}_{\infty})

will be left implicit.

Notation 2.5
.

Recall that a (strict) 2-category is called
gaunt
if every invertible 1-cell and 2-cell is an identity.
We will write
\mathrm{Gaunt}_{2}
for the full subcategory of the category of 2-categories spanned by the gaunt ones.
When we restrict to this subcategory, there
is an easy-to-construct fully faithful functor

\mathrm{Gaunt}_{2}\rightarrow\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{S}),

see
[
Rui24
, Construction 3.4]
. We will
leave this inclusion implicit. Moreover, we write

[n;m_{1},\dotsc,m_{n}]\in\mathrm{Gaunt}_{2}

for the
Joyal disk
associated to integers
n,m_{1},\dotsc,m_{n}\geq 0
.

Notation 2.6
.

There are reflective inclusions

(-)_{h},(-)_{v}:\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{S})\rightarrow\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})

which both admit right adjoints

\mathrm{Hor}(-),\mathrm{Vert}(-):\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})\rightarrow\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{S})

respectively. This has been discussed in
[
Rui23
, Subsection 2.2]
and
[
Rui24
, Notation 3.9]
. Consequently, there are two ways to extract a 2-fold Segal space from a double Segal space
\mathscr{P}
: by
taking its
horizontal fragment

\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})
or
vertical fragment

\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
. There are relations between
the above functors, namely,

X_{v}=X_{h}^{\mathrm{t},\mathrm{hop}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})=\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{hop},\mathrm{t}})

for a 2-fold Segal space
X
and a double Segal space
\mathscr{P}
.

Notation 2.7
.

Let
x,y
be objects of a 2-fold Segal space
X
. Then we will write

X(x,y)

for the Segal space of maps from
x
to
y
. This is level-wise defined by the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(x,y)_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{(x,y)\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0,0}^{\times 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0,n}^{\times 2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

for
[n]\in\Delta
. A 2-fold Segal space is locally complete if and only if all mapping Segal spaces are complete. This was shown by Haugseng
[
Hau18
]
, see also
[
Rui23
, Proposition 2.16]
.

Remark 2.8
.

If
\mathscr{P}
is a double
\infty
-category, then
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})
is always locally complete (see
[
Rui23
, Remark 2.19]
and
[
Rui24
, Remark 3.10]
). By definition (see
[
Rui24
, Table 3.1.1]
),
a double
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}
is locally complete
if and only if
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
is locally complete as well.

Notation 2.9
.

Let

\mathrm{Seg}(\mathscr{S})\subset\textup{PSh}(\Delta)

denote the full subcategory spanned by the Segal spaces of Rezk
[
Rez01
]
. I.e. it spanned by those presheaves
X:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}

which are local with regard to the spine inclusions

[1]\cup_{[0]}\dotsb\cup_{[0]}[1]\rightarrow[n],

with
n\geq 1
.
It was shown by Joyal and Tierney
[
JT07
]
that the restriction of the Yoneda embedding
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}\rightarrow\textup{PSh}(\Delta)

is the inclusion of the reflective subcategory of complete Segal spaces, i.e. those Segal spaces that are additionally local with respect to the map

J\rightarrow[0]

where
J
is the Segal set that presents the free living invertible arrow. This Segal set may be defined via the pushout square as in
[
Rui23
, Definition 2.3]
.
There is a canonical adjunction

\textup{PSh}(\Delta)\rightleftarrows\textup{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})

induced by the adjunction

\Delta^{\times 2}\rightleftarrows\Delta,

where the left adjoint is the projection onto the first coordinate and the right adjoint is the inclusion of

\Delta\times\{[0]\}
.
The adjunction on presheaf
\infty
-categories restricts to adjunctions

\mathrm{Seg}(\mathscr{S})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{S}):(-)^{(1)},\quad\textup{Cat}_{\infty}\rightleftarrows\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}:(-)^{(1)},

as one readily verifies.
We leave the (fully faithful) left adjoint inclusions implicit in this paper.

Remark 2.10
.

A 2-fold Segal space
X
is an
(\infty,2)
-category if and only if
X
is locally complete and
X^{(1)}
is a complete Segal space. This can
easily be deduced from
[
Rui24
, Remark 3.12]
.

Notation 2.11
.

As shown and discussed in
[
Rui24
]
, the
\infty
-categories
\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}
and
\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{S})
are cartesian closed. We will use the same notation
for the internal homs.
For double
\infty
-categories
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{Q}
, we write

\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}(\mathscr{P},\mathscr{Q})

for the double
\infty
-category of functors from
\mathscr{P}
to
\mathscr{Q}
. We write

\mathrm{FUN}(X,Y)

for the 2-fold Segal space of functors from
X
to
Y
.

Remark 2.12
.

It is shown in
[
Rui24
, Proposition 3.16]
that
\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}(\mathscr{P},\mathscr{Q})
is locally complete if
\mathscr{Q}
is locally complete.
Moreover, if
Y
is a locally complete 2-fold Segal space (resp. an
(\infty,2)
-category), then
\mathrm{FUN}(X,Y)
is locally complete (resp. an
(\infty,2)
-category).

Notation 2.13
.

Let
X
be a 2-fold Segal space and
\mathscr{P}
a double
\infty
-category.
Throughout this article, we will make use of both the
vertical cotensor product

[X,\mathscr{P}]:=\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}(X_{v},\mathscr{P})

and
horizontal cotensor product

\{X,\mathscr{P}\}:=\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}(X_{h},\mathscr{P})

of
X
and
\mathscr{P}
. These cotensor products were defined in
[
Rui24
, Definition 3.19]
. They interact well with
taking fragments; there are canonical equivalences

\mathrm{Vert}([X,\mathscr{P}])\simeq\mathrm{FUN}(X,\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P}))\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Hor}(\{X,P\})\simeq\mathrm{FUN}(X,\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})),

see
[
Rui24
, Proposition 3.23]
.

Remark 2.14
.

If
X
is an
\infty
-category and
\mathscr{P}
a double
\infty
-category, then the vertical cotensor product
[X,\mathscr{P}]
is computed by the composite

\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathscr{P}}\textup{Cat}_{\infty}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Fun}(X,-)}\textup{Cat}_{\infty}.

We refer to
[
Rui24
, Remark 3.24]
for an explanation.

We also recall the following definition. Firstly, note that for horizontal arrows
F:x^{\prime}\rightarrow x
and
G:y\rightarrow y^{\prime}
of a double
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}
, one may consider the two-sided
composition functor

G\circ(-)\circ F:\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}).

Then, we studied the following notions in
[
Rui23
, Definition 2.27]
and
[
Rui24
, Definition 6.5]
:

Definition 2.15
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a double
\infty
-category. Then
\mathscr{P}
is called
horizontally closed
if
G\circ(-)\circ F
is
a left adjoint for every two horizontal arrows
F
and
G
of
\mathscr{P}
. If, additionally, all horizontal mapping
\infty
-categories
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,y)
,
x,y\in\mathscr{P}
, are presentable, then
\mathscr{P}
is called
horizontally locally presentable
.

Example 2.16
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an
\infty
-category with finite limits.
Then
\mathscr{C}
is locally cartesian closed if and only if
\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathscr{C})
is horizontally closed. This observation appears
in
[
Rui23
, Corollary 7.4]
.

2.2.
\infty
-Equipments

We may expand
2.1.1
with another entry that consists of
those double
\infty
-categories that support a good notion of
formal category theory
:

Reflective subcategory

Local objects

\textup{Equip}_{\infty}

\subset

\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}^{lc}

\infty
-Equipments

The basics of the formal category theory internal to
\infty
-equipments has been studied and developed in
[
Rui23
, Section 6]
.
We will refer to this when necessary.

Recall that the definition of an
\infty
-equipment makes use of the notion of
companionships
and
conjunctions
in locally complete
double
\infty
-categories. This is a double categorical analog of adjunctions. We will not review these two notions here, but instead, refer the reader
to
[
Rui24
, Definition 4.1]
and
[
Rui24
, Definition 4.2]
for the definitions with the used terminology.

Notation 2.17
.

Suppose that
f:x\rightarrow y
is a vertical arrow of a locally complete double
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}
. Then we will denote
its companion and conjoint horizontal arrow (if they exist) by

f_{\circledast}:x\rightarrow y\quad\text{ and }\quad f^{\circledast}:y\rightarrow x,

respectively.

Definition 2.18
.

A locally complete double
\infty
-category is called an
\infty
-equipment
if it admits all companions and conjoints.

Example 2.19
.

The prototypical example of an
\infty
-equipment is the double
\infty
-category

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}

of
\infty
-categories. Its vertical arrows are given by functors and the horizontal arrows are given by

correspondences
, which may be viewed as
profunctors
or
two-sided discrete fibrations
as well, see
[
Rui23
, Section 4]
.
We will write

\textup{CAT}_{\infty}:=\mathrm{Vert}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty})

for the vertical fragment of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
. This is the model for
(\infty,2)
-category of
\infty
-categories that we will use throughout this
paper. It has been compared to Lurie’s model in
[
Rui23
, Subsection 4.3]
.

More generally, we have constructed the
\infty
-equipment

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})

of
\infty
-categories internal to an arbitrary
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
in
[
Rui23
, Section 5]
. This
\infty
-equipment is one of the key players in throughout this article. We will
review its definition in
2.4
. We write

\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}):=\mathrm{Vert}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}))

for its vertical fragment.

Convention 2.20
.

We will refer to the vertical and horizontal arrows
\mathscr{P}
in an
\infty
-equipment as the
arrows
and
proarrows
in
\mathscr{P}
respectively.

There is also another characterization of
\infty
-equipments in terms of
(co)cartesian 2-cells
.

Definition 2.21

(
[
Rui23
, Definition 3.19]
)
.

A 2-cell in a double
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}
is called
(co)cartesian
if it classifies a (co)cartesian arrow
of the source-target projection
\mathscr{P}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\times 2}
.

Proposition 2.22

(
[
Rui23
, Corollary 3.28]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a locally complete double
\infty
-category. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathscr{P}
is an
\infty
-equipment,

(2)

the source-target projection
\mathscr{P}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\times 2}
is a cartesian fibration,

(3)

the source-target projection
\mathscr{P}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\times 2}
is a cocartesian fibration.

This relied on the following verification:

Proposition 2.23

(
[
Rui23
, Proposition 3.24]
)
.

Let
f:a\rightarrow x
and
g:b\rightarrow y
be arrows of a locally complete double
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}
. Suppose that

f
admits a companion and
g
admits a conjoint. If
F:x\rightarrow y
is a proarrow
of
\mathscr{P}
, then the pasting of 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\circledast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\circledast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

is a cartesian 2-cell. Here, the left and right 2-cells are the companionship and conjunction counits respectively.
Similarly, if
G:b\rightarrow a
is a horizontal arrow of
\mathscr{P}
, then the pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\circledast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\circledast}}

is a cocartesian 2-cell. Here, the left and right 2-cells are now the conjunction and companionship units.

The class of
\infty
-equipments interacts well with taking vertical cotensor products:

Proposition 2.24

(
[
Rui24
, Corollary 4.9]
)
.

The vertical cotensor products of
\infty
-equipments are again
\infty
-equipments.

In fact, we gave a complete description of companions and conjoints in functor double
\infty
-categories in
[
Rui24
, Theorem 4.6]
using the notion of

companionable
and
conjointable
2-cells (see
[
Rui24
, Definition 4.4]
).

Example 2.25
.

As explained in
[
Rui24
, Example 3.26]
, there is an equivalence

[T^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}]\simeq\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\textup{PSh}(T))

for every small
\infty
-category
T
.

We will
make use of the following characterization of the (co)cartesian 2-cells in vertical cotensors:

Proposition 2.26
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be an
\infty
-equipment and suppose that
X
is a 2-fold Segal space. Then a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

in
[X,\mathscr{P}]
is (co)cartesian if and only if it is (co)cartesian point-wise, i.e.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{x}}

is (co)cartesian in
\mathscr{P}
for every
x\in X
.

Proof.

We will only consider the cartesian case since the other case is similar. Note that the 2-cell of
[X,\mathscr{P}]
in question is cartesian
if and only if the comparison vertical 2-cell
\gamma\rightarrow{\beta}^{\circledast}{\delta}{\alpha}_{\circledast}
is invertible in
\mathrm{Vert}([X,\mathscr{P}])=\mathrm{FUN}(X,\mathscr{P})
.
But this can be detected point-wise, so we have to check that the induced arrow
\gamma_{x}\rightarrow\beta_{x}^{\circledast}{\delta_{x}}{\alpha_{x,\circledast}}
is invertible in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
for every
x\in X
.
Here, we used that
\mathrm{ev}_{x}:[X,\mathscr{P}]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
is a functor and hence preserves cartesian 2-cells.
∎

2.3.
Gray tensor product

Throughout this article, we will make use of the model for the Gray tensor product for
(\infty,2)
-categories that was
considered in
[
Rui24
]
. This definition relied on the
squares construction
: the functor

\textup{Sq}:\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}\rightarrow\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}

that carries an
(\infty,2)
-category to the double
\infty
-category of
squares
in
\mathscr{X}
, given by

\textup{Sq}(\mathscr{X})_{n,m}=\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}}([n]\otimes_{s}[m],\mathscr{X}).

Here
(-)\otimes_{s}(-)
denotes the Gray tensor product for strict 2-categories. The joint work
[
AORR
]
is dedicated
to the study of the squares functor and its results have been used throughout
[
Rui23
]
and
[
Rui24
]
.

Definition 2.27

(
[
Rui24
, Definition 5.2]
)
.

The
Gray tensor product

\mathscr{X}\otimes\mathscr{Y}
of
(\infty,2)
-categories
\mathscr{X}
and
\mathscr{Y}
is the unique
(\infty,2)
-category with the property that

\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}}(\mathscr{X}\otimes\mathscr{Y},\mathscr{Z})\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}(\mathscr{X}_{h}\times\mathscr{Y}_{v},\textup{Sq}(\mathscr{Z})).

naturally for every
(\infty,2)
-category
\mathscr{Z}
.

Notation 2.28

(
[
Rui24
, Definition 5.6]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{X}
and
\mathscr{Y}
be
(\infty,2)
-categories. Then we will consider the vertical cotensor product

\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}):=[\mathscr{X},\textup{Sq}(\mathscr{Y})],

which has the following description:

•

its objects are functors
f:\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}
,

•

its vertical arrows
f\rightarrow g
correspond to natural transformations from
f
to
g
, i.e. maps
\alpha:[1]\times\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}

with identifications
\alpha|\{0\}\times\mathscr{X}\simeq f
and
\alpha|\{1\}\times\mathscr{X}\simeq g
,

•

its horizontal arrows
f\rightarrow g
correspond to
lax natural transformations
from
f
to
g
, i.e. maps
\alpha:[1]\otimes\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}

with identifications
\alpha|\{0\}\otimes\mathscr{X}\simeq f
and
\alpha|\{1\}\otimes\mathscr{X}\simeq g
.

Remark 2.29
.

The companions in
\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
are precisely those lax natural transformations
\alpha:h\rightarrow k
that are
strict
, i.e. those for which the 2-cells inhabiting the lax naturality squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{y}}

associated to each arrow
f:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{X}
, are invertible (see
[
Rui24
, Proposition 5.7]
). There is a locally fully faithful companion embedding

\mathrm{Vert}(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}))=\mathrm{FUN}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})\rightarrow\mathrm{FUN}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})=\mathrm{Hor}(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}))

that is the identity on objects and carries natural transformations to their associated strict lax natural transformation. This is discussed in
[
Rui24
, Section 5.1]
and
relies on the results of
[
AORR
]
(cf.
[
Rui23
, Corollary 3.13]
).

Remark 2.30
.

The
universal property of squares
that will be proven in
[
AORR
]
dictates the existence of a unique functor

\textup{Sq}(\mathrm{FUN}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}))\rightarrow\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})

that extends the counit
\mathrm{FUN}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})_{v}=\mathrm{Vert}(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}))_{v}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
. The resulting extension
has the property that it is an equivalence onto the subobject of
\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
whose horizontal arrows are companions: the strict lax natural transformations.

2.4.
Conduché modules

We will give a quick recollection of the material in
[
Rui23
, Section 5]
in slightly more generality. In particular,
we will recover the definition of the
\infty
-equipment
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
of categories internal to
\mathscr{E}
in the process.

Notation 2.31
.

For an
\infty
-category
K
, we will consider the
\infty
-category

\Delta/K:=\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/K\times_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}\Delta.

It has the feature that the projection
\Delta/K\rightarrow\Delta
is precisely the right fibration that classifies
K
, viewed as a Segal space.

Definition 2.32
.

Let
\mathscr{E}
be an
\infty
-topos. A
Conduché
K
-module internal to
\mathscr{E}
is a functor

M:(\Delta/K)^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}

so that:

(1)

for every
k\in K
, the restriction
(\{k\}_{!})^{*}M
is an
\infty
-category internal to
\mathscr{E}
(see
[
Hau18
, Section 7]
or
[
Rui23
, Definition 5.14]
),

(2)

for every arrow
f:[2]\rightarrow K
, the canonical map

\quad\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}M([n+1]\xrightarrow{0\leq 1\leq\dotsb\leq 1\leq 2}[2]\xrightarrow{f}K)\rightarrow M([1]\xrightarrow{0\leq 2}[2]\xrightarrow{f}K)

is an equivalence,

cf.
[
Rui23
, Proposition 5.3]
.
We will write

\mathrm{Con}(K;\mathscr{E})\subset\mathrm{Fun}((\Delta/K)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{E})

for the full subcategory spanned by these Conduché modules.

Remark 2.33
.

Note that the subcategories of Conduché modules are functorial. We can consider the composite functor

\textup{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\Delta/(-)}\widehat{\textup{Cat}}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Fun}(-,\mathscr{E})}\widehat{\textup{Cat}}_{\infty}

and this restricts to the Conduché modules level-wise, yielding a functor
\mathrm{Con}(-;\mathscr{E}):\textup{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\widehat{\textup{Cat}}_{\infty}
.

Remark 2.34
.

Note that the Conduché
[n]
-modules are precisely the
complete Conduché
(n+1)
-modules
of
[
Rui23
, Definition 5.14]
.
Condition (2) of
2.32
is vacuous for
n=0,1
by characterization (1) of
[
Rui23
, Proposition 5.3]
.

Construction 2.35
.

The simplicial
\infty
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{on}(K;\mathscr{E})
is obtained by restricting the functor
\mathrm{Con}(-;\mathscr{E})
along the composite

\Delta\xrightarrow{(-)^{\mathrm{op}}}\Delta\xrightarrow{-\times K}\textup{Cat}_{\infty}.

Remark 2.36
.

Note that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{on}([0];\mathscr{E})
concides with the double
\infty
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
of
[
Rui23
, Construction 5.16]
.

We may now prove the following generalization of
[
Rui24
, Corollary 5.19]
:

Proposition 2.37
.

The simplicial
\infty
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{on}(K;\mathscr{E})
is a locally complete double
\infty
-category and there is a natural equivalence

\{K^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\}\simeq\mathbb{C}\mathrm{on}(K;\mathscr{E})

for every
\infty
-category
K
.

Proof.

The demonstration provided in
[
Rui24
, Corollary 5.19]
generalizes to this situation as well. Following this strategy, it suffices to show that

\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}(X_{v}\times K_{h},\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}))\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}(X,\mathrm{Con}(K^{\mathrm{op}};\mathscr{E}))

naturally for
\infty
-categories
X
and
K
. One can rerun the proof of
[
AF20
, Theorem 1.26]
to show that

\mathrm{Con}(-;\mathscr{E})
is right Kan extended from its restriction to
\Delta
. Consequently, it suffices to check the above for
K=[n]
. In these cases,
the equivalence holds by construction. Note that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{on}(K;\mathscr{E})
is locally complete since
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
is locally complete, see
[
Rui24
, Proposition 3.16]
.
∎

3.
Lax functors and adjunctions

In this section, we introduce and study the notion of (normal) lax functors between double
\infty
-categories, as well as a suitable
notion of adjunctions between lax functors.

3.1.
Lax functors

Following Grandis and Paré
[
GP99
]
, we may extend the notions of lax functors and adjunctions
of (strict) double categories using the Gray tensor product (see
2.3
).

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{Q}
be double
\infty
-categories. A
lax functor

h:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}
between
double
\infty
-categories is a lax natural transformation

h:[1]\otimes\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\textup{CAT}_{\infty}

from
\mathscr{P}
to
\mathscr{Q}
with the property
that for any inert map
\alpha:[n]\rightarrow[m]
,
the associated lax naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{n}}

commutes. Additionally, we call
U

normal
if this condition holds for surjective structure maps
\alpha
as well.
It is called
strict
if all lax naturality squares commute.

Remark 3.2
.

In the double categorical literature, normal lax functors between (strict) double categories are also called
unitary
or
normalized
. We will stick to the terminology of Shulman
[
Shu08
]
.

Remark 3.3
.

One may also define a dual notion of
oplax functors
using
oplax
natural transformations. However, we will not need this notion
in this paper.

Remark 3.4
.

In
[
HHLN21
, Theorem 5.3.1]
, Haugseng, Hebestreit, Linskens and Nuiten prove that lax natural transformations between
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
-valued functors may be viewed
as fibered maps between their corresponding cocartesian fibrations, not necessarily preserving cocartesian arrows.
For the definition of lax natural transformations that we use in this paper (see
2.3
), this has been proven
use double categorical techniques in
[
Rui23
, Proposition 5.20]
.

This result specializes in the following fibrational perspective on lax functors between double
\infty
-categories.
If

\bar{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
and
\bar{\mathscr{Q}}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
are cocartesian fibrations that classify
double
\infty
-categories
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{Q}
, then a lax functor between
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{Q}
is precisely a functor
\bar{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\bar{\mathscr{Q}}
fibered over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
that preserves
cocartesian lifts of inert morphisms.

Notation 3.5
.

Let
h:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}
be a lax functor between double
\infty
-categories. Then we may take fibers in the commutative naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\times 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{0}^{\times 2}}

above objects
x,y\in\mathscr{P}
. We will denote this functor by

h_{x,y}:\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(h(x),h(y)).

We may construct an ambient double
\infty
-category whose objects are double
\infty
-categories,
vertical arrows are functors and horizontal arrows are lax functors:

Definition 3.6
.

The
double
\infty
-category of double
\infty
-categories

\mathbb{D}\mathrm{blCat}_{\infty}

is defined to be the sub double
\infty
-category
of the pullback

\textup{Sq}(\mathrm{FUN}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{\mathrm{in}},\textup{CAT}_{\infty}))\times_{\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{\mathrm{in}},\textup{CAT}_{\infty})}\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\textup{CAT}_{\infty})

formed using the map of
2.30
,
spanned by the double
\infty
-categories. Here
\Delta_{\mathrm{in}}
denotes the wide subcategory of
\Delta
consisting
of the inert maps.

Definition 3.7
.

We will write

\mathrm{DBLCAT}_{\infty}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathrm{DBLCAT}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{lax}}

for the vertical and horizontal
(\infty,2)
-category of
\mathbb{D}\mathrm{blCat}_{\infty}
respectively.

Note that the companions of
\mathbb{D}\mathrm{blCat}^{\mathrm{lax}}
are precisely the strict lax functors, see
2.29
. Moreover, this remark specializes in the following:

Proposition 3.8
.

There is a locally fully faithful functor

\mathrm{DBLCAT}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathrm{DBLCAT}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{lax}}

that is the identity on objects and carries a functor to its associated strict lax functor.

3.2.
Lax adjunctions

Now that we have access to ambient
(\infty,2)
-categories of double
\infty
-categories, we can make
sense of adjunctions between double functors:

Definition 3.9
.

Let
u:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}
and
v:\mathscr{Q}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
be lax functors between double
\infty
-categories. Then

(u,v)
is called a
lax adjunction
if the pair forms an adjunction in
\mathrm{DBLCAT}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{lax}}
. Moreover, we use
the following terminology:

•

the adjunction is called
normal
if both
u
and
v
are normal,

•

the adjective ‘lax’ is dropped if both
u
and
v
are strict, in which case
(u,v)
is an adjunction in
\mathrm{DBLCAT}_{\infty}
.

We have the following useful level-wise characterization of lax adjunctions:

Proposition 3.10
.

Suppose that
u:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}
is a lax functor between double
\infty
-categories. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

u
admits a lax right adjoint,

(2)

u
is strict, each functor
u_{n}:\mathscr{P}_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}_{n}
admits a right adjoint
v_{n}:\mathscr{Q}_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
,
and the mate transformation

\alpha^{*}v_{m}\rightarrow v_{n}u_{n}\alpha^{*}v_{m}\simeq v_{n}\alpha^{*}u_{m}v_{m}\rightarrow v_{n}\alpha^{*}

is invertible for every inert map
\alpha:[n]\rightarrow[m]
,

(3)

u
is strict and (2) holds for
n,m\in\{0,1\}
.

In this case,
u
is normal if and only if assertion (2) or (3) holds for the surjective maps
\alpha
as well.

Proof.

The equivalence of assertions (1) and (2) follows from Haugeng’s recognition theorem for adjunctions between lax natural transformations
[
Hau21a
, Theorem 4.6]
; this has been shown in
[
Rui24
, Corollary 5.11]
for the current
model of lax natural transformations. One readily deduces
that (2) and (3) are equivalent by using the fact that
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{Q}
satisfy the Segal conditions.
∎

Example 3.11
.

Let
f=(f^{*},f_{*}):\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}
be a geometric morphism between
\infty
-toposes.
According to
[
Rui23
, Proposition 5.17]
, the inverse image functor
induces a functor

f^{*}:=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(f^{*}):\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})

between
\infty
-equipments of internal categories, induced by post-composing with
f^{*}
. Here we crucially used
that the inverse image preserves colimits, so in particular, geometric realizations. The direct image
f_{*}
does not
generally preserve these realizations, so it does not give rise to a functor per se. However,
post-composition with
f_{*}
preserves
Segal and completeness conditions so that characterization (3) of
3.10
implies that
there exists a
normal lax
functor

f_{*}:\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{F})

right adjoint to
f^{*}
.

In the case that
f
is given by an étale geometric morphism
\mathscr{E}/e\rightarrow\mathscr{E}

for some object
e\in\mathscr{E}
, then
f^{*}
admits a further left adjoint

f_{!}:\mathscr{E}/e\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
given by projection. This projection does not preserve the terminal object, but Proposition 5.17 of
[
Rui23
]

still yields a (strict) left adjoint for
f_{*}
, denoted by

f_{!}:=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(f_{!}):\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{F}).

Definition 3.12
.

Suppose that
I
and
\mathscr{P}
are double
\infty
-categories. Then we say that
\mathscr{P}

admits all
I
-shaped limits
if the diagonal functor

\Delta_{I}:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}(I,\mathscr{P})

admits a lax right adjoint
\lim_{I}
. We say that these limits are
normal
(resp.
strict
) if
\lim_{I}
is normal (resp. strict).

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
I
be an
\infty
-category. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

\mathscr{P}
has all
I_{v}
-shaped limits,

(2)

for each
n
, the
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}_{n}
has
I
-shaped limits, and they
are preserved by the structure maps
\mathscr{P}_{m}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{n}
associated to inert map
\alpha:[n]\rightarrow[m]
,

(3)

(2) holds for
n,m\in\{0,1\}
.

Moreover, if (2) or (3) holds for the surjective maps (resp. all maps) as well, then all
I_{v}
-shaped limits are normal (resp. strict).

Proof.

This follows from the characterizations of
3.10
.
∎

Example 3.14
.

Let
\mathscr{E}
be an
\infty
-topos. Then one may use the above characterization to verify that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
has all
I_{v}
-shaped limits for every
small
\infty
-category
I
. It also admits
I_{h}
-shaped limits. To this end, we may replace
I
by
I^{\mathrm{op}}

and use the identification of
2.37
, so that we must demonstrate that the functor

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{on}([0];\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathrm{on}(I;\mathscr{E})

admits a right adjoint. In light of
3.10
, it suffices to demonstrate that for
i=0,1
the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Con}([1];\mathscr{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Con}([1]\times I;\mathscr{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Con}([0];\mathscr{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Con}(I;\mathscr{E})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i}^{*}}

is right adjointable: i.e. the horizontal arrows are left adjoints and the associated mate is an equivalence.
But the horizontal arrows are restricted from the functors

p_{j}^{*}:\textup{PSh}((\Delta/[j])^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}((\Delta/([j]\times I))^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{E}),

for appropriate choice of
j\in\{0,1\}
,
where
p_{j}:\Delta/([j]\times I)\rightarrow\Delta/[j]
denotes the pushforward. The functor
p_{j}^{*}
admits a right adjoint

p_{j,*}
that is computed by right Kan extensions along
p_{j}
. Slightly informally, it may be computed by
the formula

p_{j,*}X(f)=\lim_{g\in\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}([n],I)}X([n]\xrightarrow{(g,f)}[j]\times I)

for
X:(\Delta/([j]\times I))^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
and
f:[n]\rightarrow[j]
.
The functor
p_{j,*}
restricts to a right adjoint

\mathrm{Con}([j]\times I;\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathrm{Con}([j];\mathscr{E}).

It is not necessary to check the Conduché conditions as these are vacuous for the codomains when
j=0,1
. One can readily verify that the mate is an equivalence.

3.3.
Lax adjunctions locally

We will briefly study the effect of a lax adjunction on the mapping
\infty
-categories
of the vertical and horizontal fragments.

Proposition 3.15
.

Suppose that
u:\mathscr{P}\rightleftarrows\mathscr{Q}:v
is a normal lax adjunction between locally complete double
\infty
-categories with
counit
\epsilon
. Then the composite
functor

\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(x,v(y))\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{Q})(u(x),uv(y))\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{y}\circ(-)}\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{Q})(u(x),y)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

It will suffice to show that for each
n\geq 0
, the map of spaces

\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(x,v(y))_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{Q})(u(x),uv(y))_{n}\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{y}\circ(-)}\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{Q})(u(x),y)_{n}

is an equivalence. Let
s:[n]\rightarrow[0]
denote the unique degeneracy map. On account of
[
Rui23
, Remark 2.19]
, this composite
may be identified with the composite

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{n}}(s^{*}x,s^{*}v_{0}(y))\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{Q}_{n}}(s^{*}u_{0}(x),s^{*}u_{0}v_{0}(y))\xrightarrow{s^{*}\epsilon_{y}\circ(-)}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{Q}_{n}}(s^{*}u_{0}(x),s^{*}y).

Since
s
is surjective and
v
is normal, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{*}}

in
\textup{CAT}_{\infty}
where the top 2-cell is given by
\epsilon|[1;1]\times\{0\}
and the bottom 2-cell is given by
\epsilon|[1;1]\times\{n\}
.
Thus the whiskered counit
s^{*}\epsilon_{y}:s^{*}u_{0}v_{0}(y)\rightarrow s^{*}y
in
\mathscr{Q}_{n}
coincides with
\epsilon_{s^{*}y}:u_{n}v_{n}(s^{*}y)\rightarrow s^{*}y
,
and the above composite is given by

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{n}}(s^{*}x,v_{n}(s^{*}y))\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{Q}_{n}}(u_{n}(s^{*}x),u_{n}v_{n}(s^{*}y))\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{s^{*}y}\circ(-)}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{Q}_{n}}(u_{n}(s^{*}x),s^{*}y).

This is an equivalence since
\epsilon_{s^{*}y}
is the counit for the adjuction
(u_{n},v_{n})
at
s^{*}y
.
∎

The situation on horizontal fragments turns out to be more subtle, see also Shulman’s discussion in
[
Shu08
, Appendix B]
.
However, when we restrict our attention to equipments, we may still recover induced local adjunctions:

Proposition 3.16
.

Suppose that we have a lax adjunction

u:\mathscr{P}\rightleftarrows\mathscr{Q}:v

between
\infty
-equipments.
Let
f:u(x)\rightarrow y
and
g:u(x^{\prime})\rightarrow y^{\prime}
be arrows in
\mathscr{Q}
with
adjunct arrows
f^{\sharp}:x\rightarrow v(y)
and

g^{\sharp}:x^{\prime}\rightarrow v(y^{\prime})
in
\mathscr{P}
respectively. Then there is an induced adjunction

g_{\circledast}{u(-)}f^{\circledast}:\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,x^{\prime})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(y,y^{\prime}):g^{\sharp,\circledast}{v(-)}f^{\sharp}_{\circledast}.

Proof.

Let
F:x\rightarrow x^{\prime}
be a proarrow of
\mathscr{P}
.
In light of
2.23
, we obtain a (natural) pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(y,y^{\prime})}({g}_{\circledast}{u(F)}{f}^{\circledast},G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{Q}_{1}}(u(F),G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{Q}_{0}}(u(x),y)\times\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{Q}_{0}}(u(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{(f,g)\}}

Since the maps

d_{i}:[0]\rightarrow[1]
are inert, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i}^{*}}

in
\textup{CAT}_{\infty}
, where the top and bottom 2-cell are units of the level-wise adjunctions of
(u,v)
.
Consequently, we obtain a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{Q}_{1}}(u(F),G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{1}}(F,v(G))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{Q}_{0}}(u(x),y)\times\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{Q}_{0}}(u(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(x,v(y))\times\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(x^{\prime},v(y^{\prime})),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the horizontal maps are given by applying
v
and restricting along units. Combining this with the above,
we obtain a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(q,q^{\prime})}({g}_{\circledast}{u(F)}{f}^{\circledast},G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{1}}(F,v(G))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{Q}_{0}}(x,v(y))\times\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(x^{\prime},v(y^{\prime})).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{\sharp},g^{\sharp})}

But this pullback square also computes
\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,x^{\prime})}(F,g^{\sharp,\circledast}{v(G)}f^{\sharp}_{\circledast})
.
∎

Remark 3.17
.

In particular, we may apply
3.16
to the identity adjunction

\mathrm{id}:\mathscr{P}\rightleftarrows\mathscr{P}:\mathrm{id},

so that we obtain an adjunction

{g}_{\circledast}{(-)}{f}^{\circledast}:\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,x^{\prime})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(y,y^{\prime}):{g}^{\circledast}{(-)}{f}_{\circledast}

for every two arrows
f:x\rightarrow y
and
g:x^{\prime}\rightarrow y^{\prime}
. This was also observed in
[
Rui23
, Remark 3.34]
.

3.4.
Preservation of cartesian cells

One may already deduce that a normal lax right adjoint between
\infty
-equipments preserves all companions and conjoints using
3.16
.
But a stronger statement is true, namely, we have the following generalization of a result by Shulman in the context of (strict) double categories
[
Shu08
, Proposition 6.8]
:

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
h:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}
be a lax functor between locally complete double
\infty
-categories. Suppose
that
f:a\rightarrow x
and
g:b\rightarrow y
are vertical arrows of
\mathscr{P}
so that:

•

f
and
h(f)
admit a companion in
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{Q}
respectively,

•

g
and
h(g)
admit a conjoint in
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{Q}
respectively.

Then any cartesian cell
c
in
\mathscr{P}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

is carried to a cartesian cell in
\mathscr{Q}
by
h
.

Let us first highlight two corollaries of this result. The proof follows below.

Corollary 3.19
.

Let
h:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}
be a lax functor between
\infty
-equipments. Then
h
preserves cartesian cells.
Consequently, if
f:a\rightarrow x
,
g:b\rightarrow y
are arrows in
\mathscr{P}
then there is a natural equivalence

h({g}^{\circledast}{F}{f}_{\circledast})\simeq{h(g)}^{\circledast}{h(F)}{h(f)}_{\circledast}

for every proarrow
F:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{P}
.

Proof.

This follows directly from
3.18
and
2.23
.
∎

Corollary 3.20
.

Let
h:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}
be a normal lax functor between
\infty
-equipments. Then
h
preserves companions and conjoints, i.e.
h(f_{\circledast})\simeq h(f)_{\circledast}
and
h(f^{\circledast})\simeq h(f)^{\circledast}
for every arrow
f:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{P}
.

The proof of Shulman of
3.18
in the case of double categories is via chasing through various pastings of 2-cells.
We will see that the same proof applies in the
\infty
-categorical context as well.
First, we introduce auxiliary notation that will ease the bookkeeping involved in the proof of
3.18
.

Notation 3.21
.

Let
h:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}
be a lax functor between
\infty
-equipments. Suppose
that
\sigma:[m,0]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
is a functor. Then we may view
\sigma
as an object of the
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}_{m}
.
Now if
\alpha:[n]\rightarrow[m]
is a structure map, then
h
provides a lax naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{n}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{n}}

The component of the natural transformation at
\sigma
corresponds to a map
[1]\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}_{n}
. In turn,
this may be viewed as a functor from
[n,1]
to
\mathscr{Q}
. We will denote this by

h(\sigma,\alpha):[n,1]\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}.

Note that this functor restricts to
to
\alpha^{*}h_{m}(\sigma)
on
[n,\{0\}]
and
to
h_{n}(\alpha^{*}\sigma)
on
[n,\{1\}]
.

Remark 3.22
.

There is compatibility in the functors of
3.21
. Suppose that there is a further structure map

\beta:[i]\rightarrow[n]
. Then the lax naturality square associated with the composite
\alpha\beta

is given by the outer square in the pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{i}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{i}}

Unraveling the definitions, this means that
h(\sigma,\alpha\beta)
can be obtained by vertically pasting
the grids
h(\sigma,\alpha)\circ[\beta,1]
and
h(\alpha^{*}\sigma,\beta)
.
More precisely,
one may consider the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[i,0]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[i,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[i,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[i,\{0\}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[i,\{1\}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(\alpha^{*}\sigma,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\beta,1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(\sigma,\alpha)}

which induces a map
[i,2]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
by the universal property
of the pushout. If we restrict this map to

[i,\{0\leq 1\}]
, then this is equivalent to
h(\sigma,\alpha\beta)
.

Proof of
3.18
.

We closely follow the proof by Shulman
[
Shu08
, Proposition 6.8]
for the case of (strict) equipments.
In view of
2.23
, the cartesian cell
c
of
\mathscr{P}
decomposes as

\alpha
is an equivalence in
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(h(a),h(b))
by exhibiting an
inverse. This then proves the lemma.

Rui23
, Proposition 3.24]
.

We will verify that
\beta\alpha\simeq\mathrm{id}_{hG}
and
\alpha\beta\simeq\mathrm{id}_{G^{\prime}}
in two steps.

Let us first look at the vertical composition
\beta\alpha
. We use the defining feature of
\alpha
and the conjunction and companionship triangle identities
to rewrite this as

Note that the pasting of the top row of 2-cells is equivalent to
h(G,s_{0}s_{3})\circ[d_{2}d_{1},1]
.
Thus
3.22
implies that the total pasting of the top and bottom rows is given by
h(G,d_{2}d_{1}s_{0}s_{3})\simeq\mathrm{id}_{hG}
.

Next, we will consider the vertical composite
\alpha\beta
and show that it is equivalent to
\mathrm{id}_{G^{\prime}}
.
To this end, the universal property of the cartesian cell
c^{\prime}

implies that it suffices to verify that the pasting

c^{\prime}\alpha\beta\simeq\hbox to225.19pt{\vbox to127.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 113.0253pt\lower-62.34587pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\gamma

Note that the composite of the middle row of 2-cells is equivalent to
h(F,s_{0}s_{3})\circ[d_{2}d_{1},1]
.
It follows again from
3.22
that the composite of the middle and bottom rows is given by

h((\mathrm{id}_{x},F,\mathrm{id}_{y}),d_{2}d_{1}s_{0}s_{3})=\mathrm{id}_{hF}
. This shows that
c^{\prime}\beta\alpha
is equivalent to
c^{\prime}
, as desired.
∎

4.
Reflective, proper and smooth lax functors

In this section, we will highlight four different kinds of lax functors:

(1)

the
reflective
ones: these are generalizations of reflective inclusions of
\infty
-categories,

(2)

the
horizontally locally reflective
ones: these induce reflective inclusions on horizontal mapping
\infty
-categories,

(3)

the
proper
ones: these are functors that interact well with formal category theory, in the sense that they preserve all internal left Kan extensions,

(4)

the
smooth
ones: these are dual to (3).

We will see that (2), (3) and (4) all contain (1). We will discuss some examples as well.

4.1.
Reflective lax functors

We commence by discussing variants (1) and (2) in this subsection.

Definition 4.1
.

A lax functor
i:\mathscr{Q}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
between double
\infty
-categories is called a
reflective inclusion
if
i

is normal, fully faithful level-wise, and admits a left adjoint.

Example 4.2
.

On account of
3.11
, every geometric embedding between
\infty
-toposes gives rise to reflective inclusion between the respective
\infty
-equipments
of internal categories.

Since every
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
admits a geometric embedding
i:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\textup{PSh}(T)
into a presheaf topos for some small
\infty
-category
T
,
this particularly implies that there is a reflective inclusion

i_{*}:\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow[T^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}]

into a vertical cotensor product of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
.
Here, we used the identifications of
2.25
.
This is an important example, because this allows us to reduce questions
about the equipment
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
to the equipment
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
using the theory developed in
[
Rui24
]
. We will see this principle in action on a few occasions.

Reflective inclusions between equipments enjoy the following properties:

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
i:\mathscr{Q}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
be a reflective inclusion between
\infty
-equipments. Then
i
has the following properties:

(1)

it preserves and reflects cartesian cells,

(2)

it reflects cocartesian cells,

(3)

it preserves and reflects fully faithful arrows (see
[
Rui23
, Definition 6.1]
),

(4)

it reflects exact squares (see
[
Rui23
, Definition 6.24]
).

Proof.

Let
L
be the left adjoint to
i
. Then we have that
L_{1}i_{1}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{Q}_{1}}
and
L_{0}i_{0}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{Q}_{0}}
. Since
L
is strict, it preserves companions, conjoints and (co)cartesian cells. We have shown in
3.4

that
i
preserves companions, conjoints and cartesian cells. Now assertions (1) and (2) follow immediately from these observations.
Note that (3) follows directly from the definition of fully faithful arrows, (1) and the
fact that normal lax functors preserve the identity 2-cells associated to
vertical
arrows.

Let us show assertion (4). Suppose that we have a lax commutative square
