\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{Q})
. Then this factors uniquely as a vertical composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w}^{\circledast}{}{g}_{\circledast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}

in
\mathscr{Q}
where the bottom 2-cell is cartesian. By definition, the lax square
\alpha
is exact if and only if the top
2-cell in this factorization is cocartesian. On account of (2), it suffices to check this for its image under
i
in
\mathscr{P}
. Since the (vertical) factorization of
above is preserved by
i
and the image of the bottom 2-cell is still cartesian on account of (1), this is precisely the condition for the image
i(\alpha)
to be exact.
∎

As announced, there is also a local variant on reflective functors:

Definition 4.4
.

A lax functor
i:\mathscr{Q}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
between double
\infty
-categories is called
horizontally locally reflective
if the following two
conditions are met:

(1)

for every two
objects
x,y\in\mathscr{Q}
, the induced functor on horizontal mapping
\infty
-categories

i_{x,y}:\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(x,y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(i(x),i(y))

is an inclusion of a reflective subcategory,

(2)

for every three objects
x,y,z\in\mathscr{Q}
, the natural transformation in the lax naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(y,z)\times\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(x,z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(i(y),i(z))\times\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(i(x),i(y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(i(x),i(z))}

is a local equivalence with respect to
i_{x,z}
component-wise.

In this case and context, we will refer to the local objects and local equivalences of the reflective inclusions
i_{x,y}

as the
local horizontal arrows
and
2-cells
respectively.

Remark 4.5
.

In the context of
4.4
, the 2-cell in the lax square can be recovered as follows. The lax naturality square
associated to the face map
d_{1}
gives a natural transformation

[1]\times\mathscr{P}_{2}\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}_{1}

from
d_{1}^{*}i_{2}
to
i_{1}d_{1}^{*}
. This fits in a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}\times\mathscr{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\times 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{0}^{\times 2},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}^{\times 2}}

where the right arrow is the source-target projection, and the left arrow is given by the projection
[1]\times\mathscr{P}_{2}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{2}
followed
by
(\{0\}^{*},\{2\}^{*}):\mathscr{P}_{2}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\times 2}
.
Taking fibers above
(x,z)
, we obtain a natural transformation

[1]\times(\mathscr{P}_{2}\times_{\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\times 2}}\{(x,z)\})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(Ix,Iz).

The desired natural transformation is given by the restriction

[1]\times(\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(y,z)\times\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,y))\rightarrow[1]\times(\mathscr{P}_{2}\times_{\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\times 2}}\{(x,z)\})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(Ix,Iz).

Here we used that
\mathscr{P}_{2}\simeq\mathscr{P}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}\mathscr{P}_{1}
.

Example 4.6
.

Let
\mathscr{E}
be an
\infty
-topos.
In
7
, we will construct a horizontally locally reflective lax functor

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\textup{Cat}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}))

that acts as the identity on objects and arrows, and carries proarrows to their classifying
internal two-sided discrete fibrations
.

We have the following global characterization of horizontal locally reflective inclusions:

Proposition 4.7
.

Suppose that
i:\mathscr{Q}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
is a lax functor between double
\infty
-categories. Let
us write

j:\mathscr{Q}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\times 2}}\mathscr{Q}_{0}^{\times 2}

for the fibered functor above
\mathscr{Q}_{0}^{\times 2}
induced by
i_{1}
. Then
i
is horizontally locally reflective if the following two conditions are met:

(1’)

j
is fully faithful and admits a left adjoint fibered above
\mathscr{Q}_{0}^{\times 2}
,

(2’)

the natural transformation
[1]\times\mathscr{Q}_{2}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{1}
that sits in the lax naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}

and necessarily factors through
j
, is local with respect to
j
component-wise.

The converse statement is true if
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{Q}
are
\infty
-equipments.

Proof.

If (1’) holds, then it is readily verified that condition (2’) is equivalent to condition (2) of
4.4
.
Condition (1) of this definition is precisely the assertion that

i
is a fully faithful right adjoint on the fibers above
\mathscr{Q}_{0}^{\times 2}
. Hence, if (1’) is met then (1) follows.

In the case that
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{Q}
are
\infty
-equipments, then (1) implies (1’). This follows from the observation
that
i
preserves cartesian arrows on account of
3.19

and the fiberwise characterization of fibered adjoints between cartesian fibrations
[
Lur17
, Proposition 7.3.2.6]
.
∎

Corollary 4.8
.

Every reflective inclusion of double
\infty
-categories is horizontally locally reflective.

Proof.

Let
i:\mathscr{Q}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
be a reflective inclusion of double
\infty
-categories. Then this admits a left adjoint
L:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}
. In this case,
the composite functor

K:\mathscr{P}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\times 2}}\mathscr{Q}_{0}^{\times 2}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{1}\xrightarrow{L_{1}}\mathscr{Q}_{1}

is a left adjoint to the functor
j
of
4.7
so that
Kj\simeq\mathrm{id}
, and this is fibered above
\mathscr{Q}_{0}^{\times 2}
.
Thus condition (1’) of
4.7
is fulfilled. Now, the 2-cell filling the lax naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}

is precisely the mate

d_{1}^{*}i_{2}\rightarrow i_{1}L_{1}d_{1}^{*}i_{2}\xrightarrow{\simeq}i_{1}d_{1}^{*}L_{2}i_{2}\xrightarrow{\simeq}i_{1}d_{1}^{*},

see
[
Rui24
, Corollary 5.11]
. Thus if we apply
L_{1}
to this mate, we obtain an equivalence. This implies that condition (2’) of

4.7
is met.
∎

Our main interest in horizontally locally reflective lax functors stems from the following observation:

Proposition 4.9
.

Suppose that
i:\mathscr{Q}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
is a horizontally locally reflective lax functor. Let

W:x\rightarrow y
be a horizontal arrow and
z
be an object of
\mathscr{Q}
. Suppose that the post-composition functor

iW\circ(-):\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(i(z),i(x))\rightarrow\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(i(z),i(y))

has a right adjoint
{}^{iW}{(-)}
.
Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

the functor
iW\circ(-)
preserves local horizontal 2-cells,

(2)

the functor
{}^{iW}{(-)}{}
preserves local horizontal arrows,

(3)

the functor

W\circ(-):\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(z,x)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(z,y)

admits a right adjoint
{}^{W}{(-)}{}
as well.

In this case, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(z,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(z,x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(i(z),i(y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(i(z),i(x)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{W}{(-)}{}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{iW}{(-)}{}}

Moreover, there is a similar statement for functors of pre-composition with
W
.

Proof.

It is clear that (1) and (2) are equivalent. Recall that
the natural transformation in the lax square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(z,x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(i(z),i(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(z,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(i(z),i(y))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\circ(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces iW\circ(-)}

is a local 2-cell component-wise. Consequently,
we obtain a chain of natural equivalences

\displaystyle\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(z,y)}(W\circ F,G)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(i(z),i(y))}(i(W\circ F),iG)

\displaystyle\xleftarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(i(z),i(y))}(iW\circ iF,iG)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(i(z),i(x))}(iF,{}^{iW}{iG}{}).

for
F\in\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(z,x)
and
G\in\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(z,y)
.
From this, it follows that (2) and (3) are equivalent.
∎

Remark 4.10
.

Note that if
i:\mathscr{Q}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
is a reflective inclusion of double
\infty
-categories then
condition (1) of
4.9
is automatically met.

Corollary 4.11
.

The following two classes of double
\infty
-categories are closed under reflective inclusions:

•

the horizontally closed ones,

•

the horizontally locally presentable ones.

Corollary 4.12
.

If
\mathscr{E}
is an
\infty
-topos, then
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
is horizontally locally presentable.

Proof.

To see this, we choose a geometric embedding
i:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\textup{PSh}(T)
, where
T
is some
small
\infty
-category. Then we obtain an induced reflective inclusion

i_{*}:\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow[T^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}]
, so that the result follows from
4.11
and
[
Rui24
, Theorem 6.9]
.
∎

4.2.
Proper and smooth lax functors

We will now investigate two other classes
of lax functors that contain the reflective inclusions. The definition
makes use of the
notion of exact squares as developed in
[
Rui23
, Definition 6.24]
.

Definition 4.13
.

Let
v:\mathscr{Q}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
be a lax functor between
\infty
-equipments. Then
v
is called
proper

if it is normal and admits a left adjoint
u:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}
so that for any arrow

f:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{Q}
, the commutative naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces uv(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces uv(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces uv(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

provided by the counit of this adjunction, is exact in
\mathscr{Q}
.
Dually,
v
is called
smooth
if it is normal and admits a left adjoint
u:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}
so that for any arrow
the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces uv(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces uv(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces uv(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is exact for every arrow
f:x\rightarrow y
.

Remark 4.14
.

This terminology is chosen so that the lax functors between
the slice equipments of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
given by base change along a functor will be proper (resp. smooth) if and only if
the functor is proper (resp. smooth) in the sense of Cisinski
[
Cis19
]
. We will explain this in the next subsection.

Example 4.15
.

Every reflective inclusion of double
\infty
-categories is proper and smooth as the counits will be equivalences in this case.

The main feature of proper lax functors is that they preserve the
proarrows of cones under diagrams
(see
[
Rui23
, Definition 6.9]
) in the associated formal category theories.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
v:\mathscr{Q}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
be a proper lax functor between
\infty
-equipments. Suppose that
f:i\rightarrow x
and
w:i\rightarrow j
are arrows
of
\mathscr{Q}
so that there exists a proarrow
C
of
w^{\circledast}
-cones under
f
.
Then
vC
is a proarrow of
v(w)^{\circledast}
-cones under
v(f)
.

Proof.

This readily follows from
3.16
and
3.20
.
Let
u:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}
be the
left adjoint to
v
and write
\epsilon
for the counit of the adjunction.
Now for any proarrow

F:v(x)\rightarrow v(j)
in
\mathscr{Q}
, we obtain a of chain natural equivalences

\displaystyle\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(v(x),v(j))}(F,vC)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(x,j)}({\epsilon_{j,\circledast}}{u(F)}\epsilon_{x}^{\circledast},C)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(x,i)}(w^{\circledast}\epsilon_{j,\circledast}u(F)\epsilon_{x}^{\circledast},f^{\circledast})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(x,i)}(\epsilon_{i,\circledast}u(v(w))^{\circledast}u(F)\epsilon_{x}^{\circledast},f^{\circledast})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{Q})(x,i)}(\epsilon_{i,\circledast}u(v(w)^{\circledast}F)\epsilon_{x}^{\circledast},f^{\circledast})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(v(x),v(i))}({v(w)}^{\circledast}F,v(f^{\circledast}))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(v(x),v(i))}(v(w)^{\circledast}\circ F,v(f)^{\circledast}).

This entails that
vC
is a proarrow of
v(w)^{\circledast}
-cones under
v(f)
.
∎

The above proposition entails that proper lax functors preserve
left Kan extensions
(see
[
Rui23
, Definition 6.19]
)
and their
units
(see
[
Rui23
, Construction 6.20]
).

Corollary 4.17
.

Let
v:\mathscr{Q}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
be a
proper lax functor between
\infty
-equipments. Suppose that the 2-cell

\alpha=\hbox to73.14pt{\vbox to51.15pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 36.56932pt\lower-24.08688pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.0978pt}{-7.60277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 2.9 -10.52)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.39998pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=2.33746pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -22.15 12.45 L -10.14 5.44}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.5375pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -22.15 12.45 L -10.14 5.44}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.86348}{-0.50438}{0.50438}{0.86348}{-7.3317pt}{3.93349pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(0.86348 -0.50438 0.50438 0.86348 -10.14 5.44)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.39998pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -1.8 3.55 C -0.92 1.68 1.42 0.07 2.76 0 C 1.42 -0.07 -0.92 -1.68 -1.8 -3.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

is the unit of a left Kan extension of
v(f)
along
v(w)
in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
.

Proof.

By definition, the conjoint of
g
is the proarrow of cones under
f
weighted by the conjoint
w^{\circledast}
. Consequently,

3.20
and
4.16
imply that the conjoint of
v(g)
is the proarrow
of cones under
v(f)
weighted by the conjoint
v(w)^{\circledast}
. Thus
v(g)
is the left Kan extension of
v(f)
along
v(w)
.
The claim that the associated unit is precisely given by
v(\alpha)
may be verified by inspection using the equivalences in the proof of
4.16
.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

There are dual statements for smooth lax functors: these preserve proarrows of cones
over
diagrams and
right
Kan extensions.

4.3.
Example: change of base

The main instances
of proper and smooth lax functors are given by change of base functors. We will discuss this here and give some examples.
We first define slice double categories:

Definition 4.19
.

Let
I
be an
(\infty,2)
-category. Suppose that
f:I_{v}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
is a functor between double
\infty
-categories. Then we define the (vertical)

slice double
\infty
-category

\mathscr{P}/f
by the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}/f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[[1]\times I,\mathscr{P}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[I,\mathscr{P}]^{\times 2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{ev}_{1},\mathrm{ev}_{0})}

where the bottom functor is given by the functor that selects
f
and the diagonal functor.

Remark 4.20
.

A special case of this construction was discussed in
[
Rui24
, Section 3.3]
. Since
\infty
-equipments
are closed under vertical cotensor products and limits, it follows that slices of
\infty
-equipments are again

\infty
-equipments.

Remark 4.21
.

In the context of
4.19
, if
I
is an
\infty
-category then the slice double
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}/f
is computed
as a level-wise slice. In that case, we have
(\mathscr{P}/f)_{n}=\mathscr{P}_{n}/s^{*}f
where
s
is the degeneracy map
[n]\rightarrow[0]
.

Example 4.22
.

If
E
is an object of an
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
, then we can view
E
as a category internal to
\mathscr{E}
: as the simplicial object in
\mathscr{E}
that is constantly equal to
E
. See also
8.1
.
One may verify that there is a canonical equivalence

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})/E\simeq\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E).

Definition 4.23
.

A double
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}
is said to
admit (resp. normal, strict) pullbacks
if
\mathscr{P}
admits (resp. normal, strict) limits
shaped by the free span category
\Sigma=\{s\leftarrow\top\rightarrow t\}
.

Proposition 4.24
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a double
\infty
-category with (normal, strict) pullbacks. Then for every arrow
f:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{P}
, there is a (normal, strict) lax adjunction

f_{!}:\mathscr{P}/x\rightleftarrows\mathscr{P}/y:f^{*},

where the left adjoint
f_{!}
is the pushforward functor
\mathscr{P}/x\simeq\mathscr{P}/f\rightarrow\mathscr{P}/y
.

Proof.

Note that the equivalence
\mathscr{P}/x\simeq\mathscr{P}/f
follows from
4.21
. In particular,
f_{!}
may be identified with
(s^{*}f)_{!}:\mathscr{P}_{n}/s^{*}x\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{n}/s^{*}y
level-wise.
The result now readily follows from characterization (3) in
3.10
.
∎

Example 4.25
.

We have the following examples of double
\infty
-categories that admit strict pullbacks:

(1)

the
\infty
-equipment
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
of categories internal to an
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
(see
3.14
),

(2)

the double
\infty
-category
\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathscr{C})
of spans in an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
with finite limits.

Proposition 4.26
.

Suppose that
f
is an arrow in an
\infty
-equipment
\mathscr{P}
that admits normal pullbacks. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

the pullback functor
f^{*}:\mathscr{P}/y\rightarrow\mathscr{P}/x
is proper,

(2)

for any commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}

of pullback squares in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
, the left square is exact.

Similarly, the following assertions are equivalent:

(1’)

the pullback functor
f^{*}:\mathscr{P}/y\rightarrow\mathscr{P}/x
is smooth,

(2’)

for any commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}

of pullback squares in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
, the top square is exact.

Proof.

Our assumptions assure that
f^{*}
is always normal. Spelling out the definition,
f^{*}
is proper if and only if for every arrow
j^{\prime}\rightarrow j
in
\mathscr{P}
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}f^{*}j^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}f^{*}j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

is exact. This is precisely assumption (2). The demonstration for the smooth case is analogous.
∎

Definition 4.27
.

Let
f
be an arrow in an
\infty
-equipment
\mathscr{P}
that admits pullbacks. Then
f
is called
proper
if
the equivalent conditions (1) and (2) of
4.26
are met. Dually,
f
is called
smooth

if conditions (1’) and (2’) of this proposition are satisfied.

Example 4.28
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
. Then a functor between
\infty
-categories is proper (resp. smooth) if and only if
it is proper (resp. smooth) in the sense of Cisinski
[
Cis19
, Definition 4.4.41]
. This was observed in
[
Rui23
, Example 6.28]
.

Example 4.29
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be an
\infty
-equipment that admits normal pullbacks. If
X
is an
\infty
-category, then one can readily
check that the cotensor
\infty
-equipment
[X,\mathscr{P}]
admits normal pullbacks as well using
3.10
and
2.14
. An arrow
\alpha:h\rightarrow k
in
[X,\mathscr{P}]
is proper (resp. smooth) if and only if

\alpha_{x}:h(x)\rightarrow k(x)
is proper (resp. smooth) for each
x\in X
. This can
be deduced from
2.26
as follows. Since the evaluations

[X,\mathscr{P}]\rightarrow\mathscr{P},\quad x\in X,

jointly reflect and preserve (co)cartesian cells, an argument similar to in
4.3

shows that the evaluations jointly reflect and preserve exact squares. Then one uses that pullbacks are computed level-wise as well.

5.
Monoidal
\infty
-equipments

Suppose that we have an arrow
w:i\rightarrow j
and an object
x
in an

\infty
-equipment
\mathscr{P}
. In
[
Rui23
, Proposition 6.22]
,
we explained that a left adjoint to the restriction functor
w^{*}:\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(j,x)\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(i,x)

exists when
x
admits all left Kan extensions along
w
. In this case,
the values of the left adjoint are computed by these Kan extensions.
The goal of this section
is to obtain a similar
internal
result where the vertical mapping
\infty
-categories of
\mathscr{P}
are now replaced
by suitable internal homs in
\mathscr{P}
.

5.1.
Monoidal double
\infty
-categories

To access these internal
hom objects, we will need to introduce the notion
of a
closed monoidal structure
on a double
\infty
-category. In the strict context, this has been studied by (among others) Grandis-Paré
[
GP04
]
and Hansen-Shulman
[
HS19
]
.

Definition 5.1
.

A
monoidal double
\infty
-category
is a monoid in the
\infty
-category
\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}
.
Concretely, it is
a simplicial double
\infty
-category

\mathscr{P}^{\otimes}:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}

so that
so that the structure maps of
\mathscr{P}^{\otimes}
give rise to an equivalence

\mathscr{P}^{\otimes}([n])\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathscr{P}^{\otimes}(\{0\leq 1\})\times\dotsb\times\mathscr{P}^{\otimes}(\{n-1\leq n\})

for all
n\geq 0
.
The object
\mathscr{P}^{\otimes}([1])
is called the
underlying double
\infty
-category
of
\mathscr{P}^{\otimes}
.

Remark 5.2
.

Recall that the functors
\mathrm{Vert}(-)
and
\mathrm{Hor}(-)
preserve products, and hence lift to functors
between respective monoid
\infty
-categories. Consequently, if a double
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}
has a monoidal structure,
we obtain an induced monoidal structure on its vertical and horizontal fragments as well.

Example 5.3
.

If a double
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}
admits a monoidal structure, then there is an induced point-wise monoidal
structure on
\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}(X,\mathscr{P})
for every double
\infty
-category
X
. This follows again from the fact
\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}(X,-)
preserves products.

Notation 5.4
.

If
\mathscr{C}
is an
\infty
-category with finite products, we will write

\mathrm{Mon}(\mathscr{C})\subset\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C})

for the
\infty
-category of monoids in
\mathscr{C}
.

The following observation is useful:

Lemma 5.5
.

There is a canonical fully faithful functor

\mathrm{Mon}(\textup{DblCat}_{\infty})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Mon}(\textup{Cat}_{\infty}))

whose essential image is given by the functors
R:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mon}(\textup{Cat}_{\infty})

with the property that the composite

\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{R}\mathrm{Mon}(\textup{Cat}_{\infty})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}_{[1]}}\textup{Cat}_{\infty}

is a double
\infty
-category.

Proof.

Note that we have a canonical equivalence

\mathrm{Mon}(\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\textup{Cat}_{\infty}))\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Mon}(\textup{Cat}_{\infty})),

that is restricted from the functor
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times 2},\textup{Cat}_{\infty})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times 2},\textup{Cat}_{\infty})

induced by swapping the coordinates of
\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times 2}
.
Now, we note that the inclusion
\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\textup{Cat}_{\infty})
induces a fully faithful
functor

\mathrm{Mon}(\textup{DblCat}_{\infty})\rightarrow\mathrm{Mon}(\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\textup{Cat}_{\infty}))

whose essential
image consists of those monoids
M\in\mathrm{Mon}(\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\textup{Cat}_{\infty}))
so that the evaluations
M([n])
are double
\infty
-categories.
But this can be checked at
n=1
since double
\infty
-categories are closed under products.
Thus the above constructed equivalence
\mathrm{Mon}(\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\textup{Cat}_{\infty}))\simeq\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Mon}(\textup{Cat}_{\infty}))

restricts as described in the proposition.
∎

We will use this observation in the following result, which
offers a convenient way of obtaining monoidal structures by assembling level-wise cartesian structures:

Proposition 5.6
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a double
\infty
-category with all strict limits shaped by finite sets.
Then there exists a monoidal structure
\mathscr{P}^{\times}
on
\mathscr{P}
so that the restriction

\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathscr{P}^{\times}}\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}\subset\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\textup{Cat}_{\infty})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}_{[n]}}\textup{Cat}_{\infty}

encodes the cartesian monoidal structure on
\mathscr{P}_{n}
.

Proof.

If we view
\mathscr{P}
as a functor
\mathscr{P}:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
then the assumptions and
3.13

imply that
\mathscr{P}
factors through the
\infty
-category of
\infty
-categories with finite products and finite product preserving functors between them.
Hence, the desired result follows from
[
Lur17
, Corollary 2.4.1.9]
and
5.5
.
∎

Definition 5.7
.

In the context of
5.6
, the double
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}
is said to have all strict finite products and

\mathscr{P}^{\times}
is called the
cartesian monoidal structure
on
\mathscr{P}
.

Example 5.8
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an
\infty
-category. Note that
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/\mathscr{C}
admits all finite products. If
E
and
E^{\prime}

are fibered over
\mathscr{C}
, then their product is given by the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\times_{\mathscr{C}}E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}.}

Since Conduché fibrations are closed under base change and the composition of two Conduché fibrations is again a Conduché fibration,
we therefore deduce that the full subcategory
\mathrm{Con}(\mathscr{C})\subset\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/\mathscr{C}
of Conduché fibrations is closed under finite products. Moreover,
if
f:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is functor, then the pullback functor

\mathrm{Con}(\mathscr{D})\rightarrow\mathrm{Con}(\mathscr{C})
preserves these finite products. Consequently,

3.13
implies that

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
has all strict products. Hence
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
can be endowed with the cartesian monoidal structure.

Note that, from the perspective
of profunctors,
the product of
two proarrows
F:\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
and
G:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}

is given by the profunctor

(\mathscr{B}\times\mathscr{D})^{\mathrm{op}}\times(\mathscr{A}\times\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathscr{S}:(b,d,a,c)\mapsto F(b,a)\times G(d,c).

Definition 5.9
.

A monoidal structure
\mathscr{P}^{\otimes}
on a double
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}
is called
closed
if for all objects
x
of
\mathscr{P}
, the functor

x\otimes-:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}

admits a normal lax right adjoint

[x,-]:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}.

Example 5.10
.

The cartesian monoidal structure of
5.8
is closed. To see this, we
note that
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/\mathscr{C}
is both tensored and cotensored over
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
.
For
X\in\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
, we we have an adjunction

X\times-:\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/\mathscr{C}\rightleftarrows\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/\mathscr{C}:[X,-].

The left adjoint is given by taking fibered products with the projection
X\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
.
The right adjoint carries an
\infty
-category
E
fibered over
\mathscr{C}
to the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[X,E]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(X,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(X,\mathscr{C}),}

where the bottom map is the diagonal. This cotensor is compatible with pullbacks: if
f:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}

is a functor, then the canonical map

f^{*}[X,E]\rightarrow[X,f^{*}E]

is an equivalence for every
\infty
-category
E
fibered over
\mathscr{D}
. Recall
that
(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty})_{n}=\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/[n]
for
n=0,1
.
Hence, the above observations and
3.10
imply that
the cartesian structure on
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
is closed.

From the perspective of profunctors, the cotensor
is computed as follows. If
X\in\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
, then the cotensor
of a proarrow
F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
with
X
carries two functors

f:X\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
and
g:X\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
to the end

[X,F](g,f)=\intop\nolimits_{y,x\in X}F(g(y),f(x)).

Example 5.11
.

Taking the viewpoint of Conduché modules (see
2.4
), we may generalize
5.8
and
5.10

to conclude that that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
is a cartesian closed double
\infty
-category for every
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
.

5.2.
Kan extension arrows in monoidal equipments

Let us fix a closed monoidal
\infty
-equipment
\mathscr{P}
. If
w:i\rightarrow j
is an arrow in
\mathscr{P}
,
then we obtain a restriction arrow

w^{*}:[j,x]\rightarrow[i,x]

for every object
x
of
\mathscr{P}
. It has the defining property that it is adjunct to the composite arrow

i\otimes[j,x]\xrightarrow{w\otimes[j,x]}j\otimes[j,x]\xrightarrow{}x,

where the right arrow is the evaluation counit.
We will study left adjoints to
w^{*}
in terms of the left Kan extensions inside the formal category
theory of
\mathscr{P}

[
Rui23
, Section 6.3]
.

In the case that
w^{*}
is a right adjoint, then we may always compute left Kan extensions along
w
(if they exist) as
values of the left adjoint:

Proposition 5.12
.

Suppose that
w^{*}
admits a left adjoint

w_{!}:[i,x]\rightarrow[j,x]

in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
. If
f:i\otimes y\rightarrow x
is an arrow that
admits a left Kan extension
g:j\otimes y\rightarrow x
along
w\otimes y
,
then
g
is adjunct to the composite arrow

y\xrightarrow{f^{\sharp}}[i,x]\xrightarrow{w_{!}}[j,x].

Here
f^{\sharp}
denotes the adjunct arrow of
f
.
Moreover, the unit
f\rightarrow g(w\otimes y)
associated with this left Kan extension is adjunct to the whiskering

f^{\sharp}\rightarrow w^{*}w_{!}f^{\sharp}
of the unit
of
(w_{!},w^{*})
with
f^{\sharp}
.

Proof.

One may readily verify that there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(y,[j,x])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(y,[i,x])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(j\otimes y,x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(i\otimes y,x),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(w^{*})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(w\otimes y)^{*}}

of vertical mapping
\infty
-categories. Thus the desired result follows from
[
Rui23
, Proposition 6.22]
.
∎

We would now like to obtain a converse to the above observation and
give criteria for the existence of a left adjoint
w_{!}
in terms of the existence of left Kan extensions.
To that end, we may use
[
Rui23
, Corollary 6.6]
. This asserts that
a left adjoint to
w^{*}
exists if and only if the companion

w^{\ast}_{\circledast}:[j,x]\rightarrow[i,x]

is a conjoint as well. The main take-away of this subsection
is the following computation of the latter proarrow:

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
f:i\otimes y\rightarrow x
be an arrow of
\mathscr{P}

so that the proarrow

C:x\rightarrow j\otimes y

of
(w\otimes y)^{\circledast}
-cones under
f
exists. Then there is a cartesian 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[j,x]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[j,x]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[j,j\otimes y]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\sharp,\circledast}w^{*}_{\circledast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[j,C]}

in
\mathscr{P}
where the right vertical arrow is the unit. In particular, if
f
admits a left Kan extension
g:j\otimes y\rightarrow x
along

w\otimes y
then
f^{\sharp,\circledast}w^{*}_{\circledast}
is the conjoint of the arrow
g^{\sharp}:y\rightarrow[j,x]
adjunct to
g
.

Proof.

For brevity, we will denote the proarrow
f^{\sharp,\circledast}w^{*}_{\circledast}
by
G
. Let us write

\eta
and
\epsilon
for the unit and counit of the adjunction
(j\otimes-,[j,-])
respectively.
Applying
3.16
to the adjunction
(i\otimes-,[i,-])
, we obtain equivalences

\displaystyle\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})([j,x],y)}(F,G)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,x)}(f_{\circledast}(i\otimes F){(w^{*})^{\sharp,\circledast}},\mathrm{id}_{x})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,i\otimes y)}({(i\otimes F)}{(w^{*})}^{\sharp,\circledast},f^{\circledast}),

natural in every proarrow
F:[j,x]\rightarrow y
. In the last step, we used the observation of
3.17
.
Since the adjunct map
(w^{*})^{\sharp}:i\otimes[j,x]\rightarrow x
is given by the composite

i\otimes[j,x]\xrightarrow{w\otimes[j,x]}j\otimes[j,x]\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{x}}x,

we deduce that

{(i\otimes F)}{(w^{*})^{\sharp,\circledast}}\simeq{{(i\otimes F)}(w\otimes[j,x])}^{\circledast}\epsilon_{x}^{\circledast}.

Functors between double
\infty
-categories preserve companions and conjoints; in particular the functor

(-)\otimes(-):\mathscr{P}^{\times 2}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
. It thus follows that

{(i\otimes F)}{(w\otimes[j,x])^{\circledast}}\simeq{(i\otimes F)}{(w^{\circledast}\otimes[j,x])}\simeq w^{\circledast}\otimes F\simeq(w\otimes y)^{\circledast}{(j\otimes F)}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})([j,x],y)}(F,G)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,i\otimes y)}({{(w\otimes y)^{\circledast}}{(j\otimes F)}}{\epsilon_{x}^{\circledast}},f^{\circledast}).

By definition,
C
is the proarrow of
{(w\otimes y)}^{\circledast}
-cones under
f
, hence we obtain
an equivalence

\displaystyle\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})([j,x],y)}(F,G)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,j\otimes y)}({(j\otimes F)}{\epsilon_{x}^{\circledast}},C)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})([j,x],y)}(F,\eta_{y}^{\circledast}{[j,C]}{}).

natural in
F
. In the last step, we made use of
3.16
once again. This proves the proposition.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

In
8
, we will see an application of
5.13
to the
\infty
-equipment
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
,
where
\mathscr{E}
is an arbitrary
\infty
-topos. This then yields the existence results of functors of Kan extensions internal to
\mathscr{E}
.

We will now discuss an example where one can deduce a familiar existence result for the left adjoint to
w^{*}
in terms of Kan extensions. To this end,
we introduce an auxiliary assumption:

(
\ast
)

A proarrow
F:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{P}
is a conjoint if and only if
p^{\circledast}F
is a conjoint for every
p:1\rightarrow y
.

Here
1
denotes the unit of the monoidal structure on
\mathscr{P}
.

Example 5.15
.

The above assumption holds for
strongly pointed
(see
[
Rui23
, Definition 6.37]
) cartesian equipments such as
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
.

Corollary 5.16
.

Suppose that assumption (
\ast
) holds.
If every arrow
f:i\rightarrow x
in
\mathscr{P}
admits
a left Kan extension along
w
, then
w^{*}
admits a left adjoint
w_{!}
.

Proof.

By assumption,
w^{*}
admits a left adjoint if and only if
for every arrow
f^{\sharp}:1\rightarrow[i,x]
the proarrow
f^{\sharp,\circledast}w^{*}_{\circledast}:[j,x]\rightarrow 1
is a conjoint.
Let
g:j\rightarrow x
be the left Kan extension along
w
of the adjunction arrow
f:i\rightarrow x
. Then

5.13
asserts that

f^{\sharp,\circledast}w^{*}_{\circledast}\simeq\eta_{1}^{\circledast}[j,g^{\circledast}]\simeq([j,g]\eta_{1})^{\circledast}

where
\eta_{1}:1\rightarrow[j,j]
is the arrow adjunct to the identity
j\rightarrow j
.
∎

Corollary 5.17
.

Suppose that assumption (
\ast
) holds and that the unit
1
is the terminal object of
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
.
Let
i
and
x
be objects of
\mathscr{P}
. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

x
admits all conical colimits shaped by
i
(see
[
Rui23
, Definition 6.30]
),

(2)

the diagonal arrow

\Delta:x\rightarrow[i,x]

admits a left adjoint.

If these two equivalent conditions are met, then the left adjoint to the arrow (2) carries an arrow
f:i\rightarrow x
to its conical colimit
\mathop{colim}\displaylimits f
.

Proof.

The diagonal arrow
\Delta
is given by restriction along the terminal arrow
i\rightarrow 1
. For every
element
f^{\sharp}:1\rightarrow[i,x]
,
f^{\sharp,\circledast}{\Delta}_{\circledast}
is the proarrow of coconical cones under the adjunct arrow
f:i\rightarrow x
on account of
5.13
.
The desired result follows from this computation.
∎

6.
Tabulations and fibrations in double
\infty
-categories

The horizontal arrows of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
, i.e. the correspondences between
\infty
-categories, may equivalently be viewed as

two-sided discrete fibrations
between
\infty
-categories (we will review this notion shortly).
This fibrational perspective follows from the work of Ayala and Francis
[
AF20
, Section 4]

where they exhibit an explicit equivalence between the
\infty
-category
(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty})_{1}=\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/[1]
of horizontal arrows
and the
\infty
-category of two-sided discrete fibrations. One may find a further discussion of this equivalence in
[
Rui23
, Subsection 4.1]
.

The combined goal of the current section and
7
is to demonstrate that the situation
for
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
is an instance of a more general and purely double categorical phenomenon.
Namely, we will formulate a notion of two-sided discrete fibrations
internal to any suitable double
\infty
-category in this section.
Then we continue in
7
by
characterizing those double
\infty
-categories for which the horizontal arrows may equivalently be viewed
as these internal two-sided discrete fibrations.
For these double
\infty
-categories one may build a nice theory of two-sided discrete fibrations, including let and right fibrations as special cases.

As preparation for what follows, let us review the already established notion of two-sided fibrations between
\infty
-categories, together with an intermediate notion:

Definition 6.1
.

A span
(p,q):E\rightarrow\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{D}
of
\infty
-categories is called

regular
if the following two conditions are met:

(1)

p
is a cocartesian fibration so that its
cocartesian arrows lie over equivalences in
\mathscr{D}
,

(2)

q
is a cartesian fibration so that its cartesian arrows lie over equivalences in
\mathscr{C}
.

Remark 6.2
.

This terminology was introduced by Yoneda
[
Yon60
]
in the strict categorical setting.
The regular spans were called
curved orthofibrations
in
[
HHLN21
]
.

Definition 6.3
.

A regular span
(p,q):E\rightarrow\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{D}
between
\infty
-categories is called
a
two-sided discrete fibration
if one of the equivalent conditions of
[
HHLN21
, Proposition 2.3.13]
is met:

(1)

the functor
(p,q)
is conservative,

(2)

for each
c\in\mathscr{C}
and
d\in\mathscr{D}
, the fiber
E_{c,d}
is a space,

(3)

for each
d\in\mathscr{D}
, the functor
E\times_{\mathscr{D}}\{d\}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
is a left fibration,

(4)

for each
c\in\mathscr{C}
, the functor
\{c\}\times_{\mathscr{C}}E\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is a right fibration.

6.1.
The horizontal arrow double
\infty
-category

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a locally complete double
\infty
-category. We will commence by investigating the double
\infty
-category

\{[1],\mathscr{P}\}=\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}([1]_{h},\mathscr{P})

of horizontal arrows in
\mathscr{P}
. This object plays an important role in the formalism
of internal fibrations that we will introduce later in this section. We note the
horizontal arrow double
\infty
-category is again locally complete by
[
Rui24
, Proposition 3.16]
.

Example 6.4
.

Let
\mathscr{E}
be an
\infty
-topos.
On account of
2.37
, the double
\infty
-category of horizontal arrows in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
can be identified with
the double
\infty
-category

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{on}([1]^{\mathrm{op}};\mathscr{E})

of Conduché
[1]^{\mathrm{op}}
-modules internal to
\mathscr{E}
.

Example 6.5
.

As a simplicial
\infty
-category, the double
\infty
-category
\{[1],\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}\}

may level-wise be described as the full subcategory

\{[1],\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}\}_{n}\subset\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/([n]\times[1]^{\mathrm{op}})

spanned by the Conduché fibrations. This follows from
[
Rui24
, Corollary 5.19]
.

The double
\infty
-category of horizontal arrows comes with a projection

(\mathrm{ev}_{0},\mathrm{ev}_{1}):\{[1],\mathscr{P}\}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}\times\mathscr{P}

which enjoys the following fibrational property:

Theorem 6.6
.

The projection
(\mathrm{ev}_{0},\mathrm{ev}_{1})
induces a two-sided discrete fibration

\mathrm{Vert}(\{[1],\mathscr{P}\})(F,G)\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(x,x^{\prime})\times\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(y,y^{\prime})

between vertical mapping
\infty
-categories for every two horizontal arrows
F:x\rightarrow y
and
G:x^{\prime}\rightarrow y^{\prime}
of
\mathscr{P}
.

Remark 6.7
.

In the context of
6.6
, we will see that the cocartesian pushforward of an object
\alpha
of
\mathrm{Vert}(\{[1],\mathscr{P}\})(F,G)
,
described by a 2-cell

\alpha=\hbox to75.01pt{\vbox to52.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 37.50226pt\lower-24.17558pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

along a vertical 2-cell
\beta:f\rightarrow f^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(x,x^{\prime})
corresponds to the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\beta}

Similarly, the cartesian pullback of
\alpha
along a vertical 2-cell
\gamma:g^{\prime}\rightarrow g
is given by the pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\gamma}

This result essentially follows from double
\infty
-categorical combinatorics. Its demonstration will be in two steps: first, we will show that the above span is conservative and then show that it is regular.
Let us first start by introducing the following characterization of
locally conservative functors
:

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
f:\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}
be a functor between
(\infty,2)
-categories. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

f
is locally conservative, i.e. the induced functor
\mathscr{X}(x,y)\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}(fx,fy)
between mapping
\infty
-categories is conservative for all
x,y\in\mathscr{X}
,

(2)

the canonical map

\mathrm{Map}([1],\mathscr{X})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}([1;1],\mathscr{X})\times_{\mathrm{Map}([1;1],\mathscr{Y})}\mathrm{Map}([1],\mathscr{Y})

is an equivalence.

Proof.

This follows because the map in (2) fits in a commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}([1],\mathscr{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}([1;1],\mathscr{X})\times_{\mathrm{Map}([1;1],\mathscr{Y})}\mathrm{Map}([1],\mathscr{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}([0],\mathscr{X})^{\times 2},}

hence it will be an equivalence if and only if it is an equivalence on fibers above a pair of objects
(x,y)
of
\mathscr{X}
. But on fibers, this is precisely
the map

\mathscr{X}(x,y)_{0}\rightarrow\mathscr{X}(x,y)_{1}\times_{\mathscr{Y}(fx,fy)_{1}}\mathscr{Y}(fx,fy)_{0},

which is an equivalence if and only if
\mathscr{X}(x,y)\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}(fx,fy)
is locally conservative.
∎

Corollary 6.9
.

The span of
6.6
is conservative.

Proof.

This follows directly from the fact that the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0,1\}_{h}\times[1;1]_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0,1\}_{h}\times[1]_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]_{h}}\times[1;1]_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]_{h}}\times[1]_{v}}

is a pushout square of double
\infty
-categories, see the proof of
[
Rui24
, Proposition 3.16]
.
∎

Our next goal is to verify that the span of
6.6
is regular. To this end, we make some preparatory definitions.

Construction 6.10
.

Let
n\geq 0
, then we define the double
\infty
-category
P_{n}
by the pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{0\}_{h}}\times[1;n]_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]_{h}}\times[1;n]_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{0\}_{h}}\times[1;n+1]_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{n}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1;d_{1}]}

The canonical cofibered comparison map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]_{h}\times[1;1]_{v}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]_{h}}\times[1;1]_{v}\cup_{P_{0}}P_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]_{h}}\times[1;n+1]_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]_{h}\times[1;\{0\leq 1\}]_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{n}}

will play a role in what follows.

With this, we may formulate the following characterization of cocartesian arrows:

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
F:x\rightarrow y
and
G:x^{\prime}\rightarrow y^{\prime}
be horizontal arrows of
\mathscr{P}
. Suppose that

\alpha:[1]_{h}\times[1;1]_{v}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
classifies an arrow of
\mathrm{Vert}(\{[1],\mathscr{P}\})(F,G)
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

the arrow
\alpha
is cocartesian with respect to the projection

\mathrm{Vert}(\{[1],\mathscr{P}\})(F,G)\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(x,x^{\prime}),

(2)

the map

\mathrm{Map}({[1]_{h}}\times[1;n+1]_{v},\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}({[1]_{h}}\times[1;1]_{v}\cup_{P_{0}}P_{n},\mathscr{P})

induced by
\phi_{n}
is an equivalence on the fiber above
\alpha
for all
n
,

(3)

assertion (2) holds for
n=1
.

To prove the above lemma, we make use of the following immediate observation:

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
x,y
be objects in an
(\infty,2)
-category
\mathscr{X}
. Suppose that
\alpha:[1;m]\rightarrow\mathscr{X}
classifies an arrow

[m]\rightarrow\mathscr{X}(x,y)
. Then there is a natural pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}([n],\alpha/\mathscr{X}(x,y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}}([1;m+n+1],\mathscr{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}}([1;m],\mathscr{X}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

Proof of
6.11
.

Throughout this proof, let us write
\alpha_{0}:=\alpha|[1]_{h}\times[1;\{0\}]_{v}
and write
\beta,\beta_{0}
for the images
of
\alpha
and
\alpha_{0}
under
\mathrm{ev}_{0}
respectively.
Note that (2) is vacuous for
n=0
. Now, (1) holds precisely if the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha/\mathrm{Vert}(\{[1],\mathscr{P}\})(F,G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}/\mathrm{Vert}(\{[1],\mathscr{P}\})(F,G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta/\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(x,x^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{0}/\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(x,x^{\prime})}

is a pullback square. On account of
6.12
, this is true if and only if for every
n\geq 0
, the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}([1]_{h}\times[1;n+2]_{v},\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}([1]_{h}\times[1;n+1]_{v},\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}(\{0\}_{h}\times[1;n+2]_{v},\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}(\{0\}_{h}\times[1;n+1]_{v},\mathscr{P})}

is a pullback square after taking the fibers in each corner above the appropriate restrictions of
\alpha
.
In turn, this assertion can be precisely translated into the condition that
\phi_{n+1}
induces an equivalence above the fiber of
\alpha
. This is precisely (2).

To show that (2) and (3) are equivalent, we note that there is a canonical equivalence of double
\infty
-categories

[1]_{h}\times[1;2]_{v}\cup_{[1]_{h}\times[1]_{v}}[1]_{h}\times[1;n-2]_{v}\xrightarrow{\simeq}[1]_{h}\times[1;n]_{v}

for
n\geq 2
. One can use this to prove that the canonical map

P_{2}\cup_{[1]_{h}\times[1]_{v}}[1;n-2]_{v}\rightarrow P_{n}

is an equivalence as well. This in turn implies that the comparison map
\phi_{n}
is a pushout along
\phi_{2}
for
n\geq 2
.
∎

Lemma 6.13
.

The inclusion
i:P_{0}\rightarrow[1]_{h}\times[1;1]_{v}
admits a retraction
r:[1]_{h}\times[1;1]_{v}\rightarrow P_{0}

so that the cobase change of
\phi_{n}
along
r
induces an equivalence

r_{*}\phi_{n}:P_{n}\rightarrow P_{0}\cup_{[1]_{h}\times[1;1]_{v}}[1]_{h}\times[1;n+1]_{v}

of double
\infty
-categories. Moreover, the restriction
r|\{1\}_{h}\times[1;1]_{v}
factors through the
degeneracy map
[1;1]_{v}\rightarrow[1]_{v}
.

Proof.

Throughout this proof, we will denote the pushout-product functor for arrows in
\textup{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})
by

(-)\boxtimes(-):\mathrm{Fun}([1],\textup{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2}))^{\times 2}\rightarrow\textup{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2}).

We may express
[1;n]_{h}

as the pushout-product

[1;n]_{h}=(\{0,1\}\rightarrow[1])_{h}\boxtimes([n]\rightarrow[0])_{v}
so that we obtain the formula

[1;n]_{v}=[1;n]_{h}^{\mathrm{t},\mathrm{hop}}=([n]^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow[0])_{h}\boxtimes(\{0,1\}\rightarrow[1])_{v}.

Consequently, we may construct a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A_{n}\rightarrow[1])_{h}\boxtimes(\{0,1\}\rightarrow[1])_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces([1]\times[n+1]\rightarrow[1])_{h}\boxtimes(\{0,1\}\rightarrow[1])_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]_{h}\times[1;n+1]_{v}}}

in
\textup{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})
where
A_{n}:=[1]\times[n]\cup_{\{0\}\times[n]}\{0\}\times[n+1]
. The maps that form this pushout and the top map in the above square
are now induced by the face map

d_{n}:[n]\rightarrow[n+1].

The right arrow in this diagram is an equivalence, and the left arrow lies in the saturation of
the maps (
Seg
) and (
hc
) of
[
Rui24
, Table 3.1.1]
.

We may concretely identify
P_{0}
. Consider the simplicial subset
[2]\simeq A^{\prime}_{0}\subset[1]\times[1]
spanned by the 2-simplex

(0,0)\leq(0,1)\leq(1,1)
. Then we obtain a factorization

A_{0}\rightarrow A^{\prime}_{0}\xrightarrow{i^{\prime}}[1]\times[1].

and the left arrow is a pushout along the
\Lambda^{1}[2]\rightarrow[2]
. Consequently, the inclusion

(A_{0}\rightarrow[1])_{h}\boxtimes(\{0,1\}\rightarrow[1])_{v}\rightarrow(A^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow[1])_{h}\boxtimes(\{0,1\}\rightarrow[1])_{v}

lies in the saturation of (
Seg
) of
[
Rui24
, Table 3.1.1]
. Since the right-hand side is a double
\infty
-category, we may
assume that this is precisely
P_{0}
. Now, we
the desired retraction
r:[1]_{h}\times[1;1]_{v}\rightarrow P_{0}
is induced by the (unique) fibered retraction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}\times[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}

of
i^{\prime}
that carries
(1,0)
to
(1,1)
.

The proposition follows if we manage to show that the map

(A_{1}\cup_{[1]\times[1]}A^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow[1])_{h}\boxtimes(\{0,1\}\rightarrow[1])_{v}\rightarrow([1]\times[2]\cup_{[1]\times[1]}A^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow[1])_{h}\boxtimes(\{0,1\}\rightarrow[1])_{v}

lies in the saturation of (
Seg
) of
[
Rui24
, Table 3.1.1]
. To this end, let us write

q:[1]\times[2]\rightarrow[1]\times[2]\cup_{[1]\times[1]}A^{\prime}_{0}=:B_{1}

for the canonical (quotient) map of simplicial sets.
Recall that
[1]\times[2]
is generated by the 3-simplices

\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}
that correspond to the shuffles
(0,0)\leq(0,1)\leq(0,2)\leq(1,2)
,

(0,0)\leq(0,1)\leq(1,1)\leq(1,2)
and
(0,0)\leq(1,0)\leq(1,1)\leq(1,2)
respectively. The images
under
q
are still non-degenerate and also generate
B_{1}
. Let
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
denote the
2-simplices
(0,0)\leq(1,1)\leq(1,2)
and
(1,0)\leq(1,1)\leq(1,2)
.
Let
F_{a,b}\subset B_{1}
denote the smallest simplicial subset containing
A_{1}

together with the simplices
q(\tau_{1}),\dotsc,q(\tau_{b})

and
q(\sigma_{1}),\dotsc,q(\sigma_{b})
.

With these definitions, we obtain a filtration

A_{1}\cup_{[1]\times[1]}A^{\prime}_{0}=F_{0,0}\rightarrow F_{1,0}\rightarrow F_{2,0}\rightarrow F_{2,1}\rightarrow F_{2,2}\rightarrow F_{2,3}=B_{1}

such that:

(1)

the inclusions
F_{0,0}\rightarrow F_{1,0}
and
F_{1,0}\rightarrow F_{2,0}
are pushouts along
\Lambda^{1}[2]\rightarrow[2]
,

(2)

the inclusion
F_{2,0}\rightarrow F_{2,1}
is a pushout along
\Lambda^{2}[3]\rightarrow[3]
,

(3)

the inclusions
F_{2,1}\rightarrow F_{2,2}
and
F_{2,2}\rightarrow F_{2,3}
are both pushouts along
\Lambda^{3}[3]\rightarrow[3]

so that the restrictions to
\{2\leq 3\}
select the same degenerate edge of
B_{1}
.

All five maps are pushouts of maps that are local to the (1-dimensional) Segal spaces in
\textup{PSh}(\Delta)
(see
2.9
). For (1), this follows by definition.
For (2), this follows from
[
JT07
, Lemma 3.5]
. For (3), this can be deduced from the proof of
[
Rez01
, Lemma 11.10]
.
It now follows that the total composite
A_{1}\cup_{[1]\times[1]}A^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow B_{1}
is local with regard to the inclusion
\mathrm{Seg}(\mathscr{S})\subset\textup{PSh}(\Delta)
.

Consequently, the horizontal arrows in the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A_{1}\cup_{[1]\times[1]}A^{\prime}_{0})_{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B_{1})_{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}_{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}_{h}}

are contained in the saturation of (
Seg
) on account of the discussion in
2.9
, and the fact that
(-)_{h}
preserves
the equivalences local to the inclusion
\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{S})\subset\textup{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})
. Now, we will use that the product bifunctor of
\textup{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})
preserves
the morphisms in the saturation of (
Seg
) in both variables, see
[
Rui24
, Proposition 3.16]
and
[
Rui24
, Lemma 2.12]
.
This implies that the pushout-product bifunctor
(-)\boxtimes(-)
carries morphisms level-wise contained
in the saturation of (
Seg
), in both variables, to morphisms in the saturation of (
Seg
).
In particular,
when we view the above square as an arrow
in
\mathrm{Fun}([1],\textup{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2}))
and apply the functor
(-)\boxtimes(\{0,1\}\rightarrow[1])_{v}
to it, we will remain in the saturation of (
Seg
).
∎

Proof of
6.6
.

It remains to show that the span of the theorem is regular. We will only show property (1) of
6.1
since property (2) is formally dual.
Namely, it may
be obtained from (1) by looking at the horizontal opposite
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{hop}}
of
\mathscr{P}
, for which
the horizontal arrow double
\infty
-category is computed as
\{[1],\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{hop}}\}=\{[1]^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{P}\}^{\mathrm{hop}}
.

Let

i
and
r
be as in
6.13
. Suppose that
\beta:\{0\}_{h}\times[1;1]_{v}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
classifies an arrow of
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(x,x^{\prime})
and
\alpha_{0}:[1]_{h}\times[1]_{v}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}

classifies object of
\mathrm{Vert}(\{[1],\mathscr{P}\})(F,G)
, so that
\beta_{0}:=\{0\}_{h}\times[1]_{v}
coincides with the restriction of
\alpha_{0}

to
\{0\}_{h}\times[1;\{0\}]_{v}
. Consider the candidate
\alpha
defined by the composite

[1]_{h}\times[1;1]_{v}\xrightarrow{r}P_{0}\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathscr{P},

where the second map
\alpha^{\prime}
is the unique map determined by
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta
. Note that
\alpha|\{1\}_{v}\times[1;1]_{v}
is an equivalence in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(y,y^{\prime})
on account of the property
of the retract
r
exhibited in
6.13
.
It remains to show that
\alpha
is cocartesian. To this end, we will make use of the characterization of
6.11
: we have to show that the map

\phi_{n}^{*}:\mathrm{Map}({[1]_{h}}\times[1;n+1]_{v},\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}({[1]_{h}}\times[1;1]_{v}\cup_{P_{0}}P_{n},\mathscr{P})

induced by
\phi_{n}
is an equivalence at the fiber above
\alpha
. But this is precisely the fiber of the map

(r_{*}\phi_{n})^{*}:\mathrm{Map}(P_{0}\cup_{[1]_{h}\times[1;1]_{v}}[1]_{h}\times[1;n+1]_{v},\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}(P_{n},\mathscr{P})

induced by the cobase change
r_{*}\phi_{n}
of
\phi_{n}
at the fiber above
\alpha^{\prime}
. But
r_{*}\phi_{n}
is an equivalence on account of
6.13
so that the result follows.
∎

Remark 6.14
.

The vertical mapping
\infty
-categories of
\{[1],\mathscr{P}\}
are usually hard to access. There are some cases one can say more. Namely,
one may compute that

\mathrm{Vert}(\{[1],\mathscr{P}\})(\mathrm{id}_{x},\mathrm{id}_{y})\simeq\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(x,y))

for
x,y\in\mathscr{P}
. However, we will not give a demonstration of this here.

6.2.
Tabulations

To define the notion of two-sided discrete fibrations internal to a double
\infty
-category, we will make use of the
previously discussed horizontal arrow double
\infty
-category and one final ingredient: Grandis’ and Paré’s notion of
tabulations

[
GP99
]
,
[
GP17
]
.

Definition 6.15
.

We say that a locally complete double
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}
is
tabular
if it admits
limits shaped by the free horizontal arrow
[1]_{h}
. That is, whenever the diagonal functor

\Delta:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\{[1],\mathscr{P}\}

admits a lax right adjoint
t:\{[1],\mathscr{P}\}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
. A
tabulation
for a horizontal arrow
F:x\rightarrow y
is the
data of an object
e
of
\mathscr{P}

together with a so-called
tabulating 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

in
\mathscr{P}
that defines a counit for the adjunction
(\Delta,t)
: the composite map

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(z,e)\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{1}}(\mathrm{id}_{z},\mathrm{id}_{e})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{1}}(\mathrm{id}_{z},F)

should be an equivalence for any object
z
of
\mathscr{P}
. In particular, we then have that
e\simeq tF
.

Remark 6.16
.

There is a dual notion of
cotabulations
in
\mathscr{P}
, these are precisely tabulations in the vertical opposite

\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{vop}}
.

Remark 6.17
.

Originally, tabulations were termed
tabulators
by Grandis and Paré. We will use the terminology of Koudenburg
[
Kou14
]

that is also used in the 2-categorical literature
[
CKS84
]
.

Example 6.18
.

The
\infty
-equipment
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
associated to an
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
is tabular in light of
3.14
.

Example 6.19
.

If
F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}

is a proarrow between
\infty
-categories given by a correspondence
E\rightarrow[1]
, then its tabulating 2-cell is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}_{/[1]}([1],E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}_{/[1]}([1],E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

that is classified by the canonical map of correspondences

[1]\times\mathrm{Fun}_{/[1]}([1],E)\rightarrow E.

The span

\mathscr{C}\leftarrow\mathrm{Fun}_{/[1]}([1],E)\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is precisely the two-sided discrete fibration associated to the correspondence
E\rightarrow[1]
under the identifications discussed in
[
Rui23
, Section 4.1]
, see also the work of Ayala-Francis
[
AF20
, Section 4.1]
.
The cotabulation of
F
is given by the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

where the vertical arrows are inclusions of fibers, corresponding to the map of correspondences
E\rightarrow[1]\times E
.

Definition 6.20
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a tabular locally complete double
\infty
-category. Then
a span

(p,q):e\rightarrow x\times y

in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}
is called a
two-sided discrete fibration
(internal to
\mathscr{P}
)
if there exists a 2-cell of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

in
\mathscr{P}
that is tabulating.

Remark 6.21
.

There are more strategies for defining suitable generalizations of two-sided discrete fibrations of
(\infty-)
categories. In a stricter context,
this was done by Street
[
Str74
]
where the candidates internal to a suitable
2-category

\mathscr{X}
are introduced:

(1)

the so-called
covering spans
in
\mathscr{X}
,

(2)

the more general notion of
discrete split bifibrations
in
\mathscr{X}
, this is a representable notion: a span is a discrete split bifibration if and only if
it induces a two-sided discrete fibration on mapping categories.

There is no discrepancy between definitions (1) and (2) if
\mathscr{X}
is given by the 2-category of categories (cf.
[
Str80
, Remark 3.44]
,
[
CJSV94
, Proposition 4.10]
),
but this is generally not the case.

One can interpret our definition as a double
\infty
-categorical variant on definition (1). In the 2-categorical literature,
usually approach (2) is taken to define two-sided discrete fibrations. We depart from this strategy as this definition would only depend on the vertical fragment of the double
\infty
-categories that we consider. We stress
that our definition of two-sided discrete fibrations depends on the horizontal categorical direction as well.

The following result shows that two-sided discrete fibrations internal to a suitable double
\infty
-category are also two-sided discrete fibrations representably:

Proposition 6.22
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a tabular locally complete double
\infty
-category. If
(p,q):e\rightarrow x\times y

is a two-sided discrete fibration in
\mathscr{P}
, then

\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(z,e)\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(z,x)\times\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(z,y)

is a two-sided discrete fibration for every
z\in\mathscr{P}
.

Proof.

By assumption, there exists a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

that forms a counit for the adjunction
(\Delta,t)
so that

\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(z,e)\simeq\mathrm{Vert}(\{[1],\mathscr{P}\})(\mathrm{id}_{z},F).

The desired result can now be deduced from
6.6
.
∎

Some double functors interact well with tabulations:

Proposition 6.23
.

Normal lax right adjoint functors between tabular locally complete double
\infty
-categories
preserve tabulating 2-cells, and, in particular, preserve two-sided discrete fibrations.

Proof.

Let
v:\mathscr{Q}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
be such a right adjoint with left adjoint
u:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}
.
Suppose that
\alpha
is a tabulating 2-cell in
\mathscr{Q}
, i.e. it is
given by a morphism
\mathrm{id}_{e}\rightarrow F
in
\mathscr{Q}_{1}
as in
6.15
. Since
v
is normal,
v(\alpha)
is a morphism
\mathrm{id}_{v(e)}\simeq v(\mathrm{id}_{e})\rightarrow vF
in
\mathscr{P}_{1}
. Moreover, by normality,
the composite

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(z,v(e))\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{1}}(\mathrm{id}_{z},\mathrm{id}_{v(e)})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{1}}(\mathrm{id}_{z},vF)

is equivalent to the composite

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{Q}_{0}}(u(z),e)\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{Q}_{1}}(\mathrm{id}_{u(z)},\mathrm{id}_{e})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{Q}_{1}}(\mathrm{id}_{u(z)},F),

cf.
3.15
. The latter composite map is an equivalence by assumption.
∎

Proposition 6.24
.

Let
f:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}
be a geometric morphism between
\infty
-toposes. Then the functor
f^{*}
and the lax functor
f_{*}
(see
3.11
) preserve tabulating 2-cells and
two-sided discrete fibrations.

Proof.

Since
f_{*}
is a normal lax right adjoint, this case follows from
6.23
. For the case of

f^{*}
, note that the relevant tabulations are computed level-wise by limits shaped by the finite sets
\mathrm{Map}_{\Delta}([n],[1])
,
[n]\in\Delta
, in light of
3.14
. Such limits
are preserved by inverse image functors.
∎

Moreover, we have the following closure property:

Proposition 6.25
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a tabular locally complete double
\infty
-category. If
X
is an
\infty
-category, then the vertical cotensor product

[X,\mathscr{P}]
is again tabular and locally complete. Moreover, a 2-cell

\alpha=\hbox to73.71pt{\vbox to43.59pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 36.85487pt\lower-21.79718pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
