\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

in
[X,\mathscr{P}]
is tabulating if and only if the evaluation
\alpha_{\xi}
is a tabulating 2-cell in
\mathscr{P}
for all
\xi\in X
.

Proof.

The locally complete assertion follows from
[
Rui24
, Proposition 3.16]
. The tabular part and the characterization of tabulations may be deduced from
3.10
,
2.14
and the
fact that
\mathrm{Fun}(X,-)
preserves adjunctions.
∎

7.
Fibrational
\infty
-equipments

The goal of this section
is to axiomatize and study a class of
\infty
-equipments that support a good theory of fibrations. The key feature of these equipments should be
that there is no information loss when passing from proarrows to their associated two-sided discrete fibrations.

7.1.
Span representations

We will demonstrate that one can pass from proarrows to two-sided discrete fibrations in a coherent fashion:

Theorem 7.1
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a tabular locally complete double
\infty
-category with all pullbacks. Then there exists a lax
functor

\rho:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)})

that lifts the identity functor on
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}
and carries each horizontal arrow
F:x\rightarrow y
to the span
(p,q):e\rightarrow x\times y
associated to its tabulating 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

Moreover, if
\mathscr{P}
is an
\infty
-equipment, then
\rho
is locally horizontally reflective if and only if the following conditions are met:

(1)

all tabulating 2-cells are cocartesian,

(2)

for two composable proarrows
F:x\rightarrow y
and
G:y\rightarrow z
in
\mathscr{P}

with (cocartesian) tabulating 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

the composite of the two 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\times_{y}e^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\times_{y}e^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\times_{y}e^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

obtained by whiskering these tabulating 2-cells with the projections
e\times_{y}e^{\prime}\rightarrow e
and
e\times_{y}e^{\prime}\rightarrow e^{\prime}
, is again cocartesian.

In this case, the right adjoint to the fully faithful functor

\rho_{x,y}:\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}/(x\times y)

carries a span
(p,q):e\rightarrow x\times y
to the cocartesian pushforward
{q}_{\circledast}{p}^{\circledast}
.

Definition 7.2
.

The functor
\rho
from
7.1
will be called the
span representation
.

Remark 7.3
.

The construction of span representations for strict double categories
has been studied by Grandis and Paré
[
GP17
]
and Niefield
[
Nie12
]
. Condition (1) of
7.1
has been
considered earlier by Koudenburg
[
Kou14
]
for strict equipments.

We defer the proof of the theorem to the next subsection. Let us first study the class of equipments with horizontally locally reflective span representations.

Definition 7.4
.

An
\infty
-equipment
\mathscr{P}
is called
fibrational
if it is tabular and admits all pullbacks, and
conditions (1) and (2) of
7.1
are met.

The prototypical example of a fibrational
\infty
-equipment that we will study in this paper, is the following:

Proposition 7.5
.

The
\infty
-equipment
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
is fibrational for every
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
, and hence
it admits a horizontally locally reflective span representation

\rho:\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\textup{Cat}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})).

Remark 7.6
.

For
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{S}
, this gives a double categorical answer to a question posed by Ayala and Francis
[
AF20
, Problem 0.17]
about the
existence of such a functor.

To prove
7.5
for all
\infty
-toposes, we can reduce to the prototypical
\infty
-topos of spaces. We will need the following observations:

Lemma 7.7
.

Suppose that
(p,q):E\rightarrow\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{D}
is a span of
\infty
-categories.
Let us write
P\rightarrow[1]
for the cocomma correspondence that is defined by the pushout

P:=\mathscr{C}\cup_{\{1\}\times E}[1]\times E\cup_{\{0\}\times E}\mathscr{D}.

Then the following is true:

(1)

the canonical lax square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

is exact,

(2)

there is a canonical map
E_{c,d}\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{P}(d,c)

which exhibits
\mathrm{Map}_{P}(d,c)
as the classifying space of the fiber
E_{c,d}
if
(p,q)
is regular.

Proof.

Let us first show (1). To show exactness
[
Rui23
, Definition 6.24]
,
we first produce a (essentially unique) factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\circledast}p^{\circledast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

of the lax square in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
, where the top 2-cell is cocartesian. Then we must show that the bottom 2-cell is cartesian.
From the viewpoint of correspondences, this factorization is supplied by the composite

[1]\times E\rightarrow P\rightarrow[1]\times P

in
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/[1]
. This follows because the first arrow classifies a cocartesian arrow of the source-target projection
(\{1\},\{0\})^{*}:\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/[1]\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}^{\times 2}
of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
, as one can verify using the universal property of the pushout.
We thus have to show that the arrow
P\rightarrow[1]\times P
is cartesian with respect to
(\{1\},\{0\})^{*}
. This entails verifying that the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/[1]}(X,P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}(X,P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}(X_{1},\mathscr{C})\times\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}(X_{0},\mathscr{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}(X_{1},P)\times\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}(X_{0},P)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}\times j_{*}}

is a pullback square for every correspondence
X\rightarrow[1]
. Note that the bottom arrow
may be identified with the arrow

\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/[1]}(X_{1},P)\times\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/[1]}(X_{0},P)\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}(X_{1},P)\times\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}(X_{0},P)

induced by the forgetful functor.

In fact, we may show that the above diagram is a pullback square for any correspondence

P
from
\mathscr{C}
to
\mathscr{D}
. To verify this, it will be sufficient to check for
\sigma:[n]\rightarrow[1]
in
\Delta/[1]
, since there is fully faithful inclusion
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/[1]\rightarrow\textup{PSh}(\Delta)/[1]\simeq\textup{PSh}(\Delta/[1])
, see the proof of
[
Rui23
, Proposition 5.19]
for instance.
In this case, the above square fits as the top square in a commutative cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{/[1]}([n],P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}([n],P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{/[1]}([n]_{1}\sqcup[n]_{0},P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}([n]_{1}\sqcup[n]_{0},P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}([n],[1])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}([n]_{1}\sqcup[n]_{0},[1]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\sigma\}}

Here
[n]_{1}
and
[n]_{0}
denote the fibers of
\sigma
above
1
and
0
respectively.
The front and back squares are pullback squares. The bottom square is clearly a pullback square as well, hence the result follows.

Let us show (2). Suppose that
(p,q)
is regular.
Consider the following lax commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{c,d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\times_{\mathscr{D}}\{d\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{d\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{c\}\times_{\mathscr{C}}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{c\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

of
\infty
-categories. This produces a mate transformation

E_{c,d}[E_{c,d}^{-1}]\times(-)\rightarrow\{c\}^{*}i^{*}j_{!}\{d\}_{!}(-)

between endofunctors on
\mathscr{S}=\mathrm{Fun}(\{d\},\mathscr{S})
. Evaluating at the point
\ast
, we obtain a canonical map

E_{c,d}[E_{c,d}^{-1}]\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{P}(d,c)
. On account of
[
Rui23
, Corollary 6.27]
, the mate is an equivalence if the outer lax square in the above diagram
is exact. But the right bottom square is exact by assertion (1). To show that the
bottom pullback square is exact, it suffices to verify that
p
is proper
(see
[
Rui23
, Example 6.28]
). But this follows from
the assumption that
p
is cocartesian and
[
Lur09
, Proposition 4.1.2.15]
.
1
1

1

We will use the proper/smooth terminology of Cisinski
[
Cis19
]
. Unfortunately, this is precisely the opposite of the conventions used by Lurie
[
Lur09
]
.

Similarly, we can show that the outer (pullback) square of the two top squares is exact.
This follows from the fact that the composite
\{c\}\times_{\mathscr{C}}E\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is cartesian by
[
HHLN21
, Proposition 2.3.3]
, hence smooth.
From this, the result follows.
∎

Proof of
7.5
.

We may pick a geometric embedding
i:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\textup{PSh}(T)
for some small
\infty
-category
T
.
This induces a reflective lax inclusion

i_{*}:\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow[T^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}].

Now in light of
6.24
and
4.3
, it is necessary and sufficient to show that

[T^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}]
is fibrational. In turn,
6.25
and
2.26

imply that we may reduce to showing that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
is fibrational.

In this case, condition (1) of
7.1
has been demonstrated
by Lurie in
[
Lur17
, Proposition B.3.17]
and by Ayala-Francis in
[
AF20
, Lemma 4.1]
, where it is shown that the canonical map

E_{1}\cup_{[1]\times E_{1}}[1]\times\mathrm{Fun}_{/[1]}([1],E)\cup_{[1]\times E_{0}}E_{0}\rightarrow E

is an equivalence for every correspondence
E\rightarrow[1]
.
Condition (2) is precisely the assertion that the canonical map

E_{2}\cup_{[1]\times E_{2}}[1]\times\mathrm{Fun}_{/[2]}([2],E)\cup_{[1]\times E_{0}}E_{0}\rightarrow d_{1}^{*}E

is an equivalence in
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/[1]
for every Conduché fibration
E\rightarrow[2]
. By the above,
this is true precisely when the functor

E_{2}\cup_{[1]\times E_{2}}[1]\times\mathrm{Fun}_{/[2]}([2],E)\cup_{[1]\times E_{0}}E_{0}\rightarrow E_{2}\cup_{[1]\times E_{2}}[1]\times\mathrm{Fun}_{/[1]}([1],d_{1}^{*}E)\cup_{[1]\times E_{0}}E_{0}

is an equivalence. It is clearly essentially surjective,
so it suffices to show that it is fully faithful. This follows directly from the computation
(2) of

7.7
and characterization (6) of
[
AFR18
, Lemma 5.16]
.
∎

The span representations can be used to obtain the following double
\infty
-categorical version of a theorem of Carboni-Kasangian-Street
[
CKS84
, Theorem 4]
about recognizing span bicategories.

Corollary 7.8
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a double
\infty
-category. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathscr{P}
is equivalent to
\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathscr{C})
, where
\mathscr{C}
is some
\infty
-category with pullbacks,

(2)

\mathscr{P}
is a fibrational
\infty
-equipment and every span in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}
is a two-sided discrete fibration (w.r.t.
\mathscr{P}
),

(3)

the span representation for
\mathscr{P}
is strict and an equivalence.

Proof.

It is clear that (3) implies (1) and it is straightforward to verify that every double
\infty
-category of spans meets condition (2). It remains to show that (2) implies (3).
Let

\rho:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)})

be the span representation for
\mathscr{P}
. Then
\rho_{0}
is the identity and
\rho_{1}
is fully faithful in light of
4.7
.
It is essentially surjective by the assumption that every span in
\mathscr{P}
is a two-sided discrete fibration. Thus
\rho_{1}
is an equivalence. Now

4.7
implies that the lax naturality square associated to
\alpha=d_{1}:[1]\rightarrow[2]
strictly commutes.
In turn, one can then inductively show that the lax naturality square associated with every face map
\alpha
in
\Delta
commutes. Similarly,
one shows that span representation is also normal, since for every
x\in\mathscr{P}
, the span

x\rightarrow x\times x
is two-sided discrete. Thus
\rho
is strict.
∎

7.2.
The existence of span representations

To prove
7.1
, we will formulate a technical and intermediate step that takes care of the construction of the span representation. We will make use of a certain universal property for mapping into span double categories
exhibited by Haugseng
[
Hau21b
]
.

Lemma 7.9
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a double
\infty
-category with the property that
\mathscr{P}_{0}
admits pullbacks
and the structure map
\mathscr{P}_{0}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{1}
admits a right adjoint. Then there exists a lax functor

\rho:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)})

so that:

(1)

The functor
\rho_{0}
can be identified with the identity functor.

(2)

If we have a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

in
\mathscr{P}
that is tabulating, then
\rho
carries
F

to the span
(p,q):e\rightarrow x\times y
.

(3)

If we have tabulating 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GF}

in
\mathscr{P}
, then the lax comparison 2-cell

e\times_{y}e^{\prime}\rightarrow e^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}/(x\times z)
provided by
\rho
is obtained by applying
the universal property of tabulating 2-cells to
the composite 2-cell
\mathrm{id}_{e\times_{y}e^{\prime}}\rightarrow G\circ F
described in condition (3) of
7.4
.

Proof.

We will provide a construction of the functor
\rho
from the fibrational perspective of
3.4
.
Consider the canonical inclusion
l:\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}_{v}=(\mathscr{P}_{0})_{v}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
of the underlying vertical
\infty
-category. Passing to unstraightenings, this gives rise
to a map

l:\mathscr{P}_{0}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Un}(\mathscr{P})

of cocartesian fibrations over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
. On account of
[
Lur17
, Proposition 7.3.2.6]
, this is a left adjoint in
\textup{CAT}_{\infty}/\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
if and only if
for every
n
, the degeneracy functor
l_{n}:\mathscr{P}_{0}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{n}
induced by the terminal map
[n]\rightarrow[0]
is a left adjoint. We claim that this is the case.
Note that
l_{0}
is trivially a left adjoint (it is the identity) and that
l_{1}
is a left adjoint by assumption.

Let
i:\mathbb{G}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
denote the inclusion of the full subcategory spanned by
[0]
and
[1]
. By the same reasoning, there exists a right adjoint

i^{*}\mathrm{Un}(\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{0}\times\mathbb{G}^{\mathrm{op}}
for
i^{*}l
in
\textup{CAT}_{\infty}/\mathbb{G}^{\mathrm{op}}
.
Let us write
s:i^{*}\mathrm{Un}(\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{0}
for this adjoint followed by the projection
\mathscr{P}_{0}\times\mathbb{G}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{0}
.
We then obtain a natural equivalence

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Un}(\mathscr{P})}(x,y)=\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Un}(\mathscr{P})}(l_{0}(x),y)\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(x,s(y))\times\mathrm{Map}_{\mathbb{G}^{\mathrm{op}}}([0],[n])=\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(x,s(y))

for
x\in\mathscr{P}_{0}
and
y\in\mathscr{P}_{n}
with
n=0,1
. We will use this to show that
l_{n}
admits a left adjoint.

Suppose that
y\in\mathscr{P}_{n}
. Then we have to show that the presheaf

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{n}}(l_{n}(-),y):\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}

is representable. We may construct a natural transformation
(-)\rightarrow l_{n}(-)
in
\mathrm{Un}(\mathscr{P})
that, component-wise,
consists of
cocartesian lifts over the arrow
[0]\rightarrow[n]
in
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
. Then, for
x\in\mathscr{P}_{0}
, we obtain a natural commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{n}}(l_{n}(x),y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Un}(\mathscr{P})}(l_{n}(x),y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Un}(\mathscr{P})}(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\mathrm{id}_{[n]}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}([n],[n])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}([0],[n])}

in which every square is a pullback square.
Since
\mathscr{P}
is a double
\infty
-category, we have a further pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Un}(\mathscr{P})}(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim_{i:[k]\rightarrow[n]\in(\mathbb{G}/[n])^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Un}(\mathscr{P})}(x,i^{*}y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}([0],[n])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim_{i:[k]\rightarrow[n]\in(\mathbb{G}/[n])^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Map}_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}([0],[k]),}

see item (iii) of
[
Hau21b
, Definition 2.6]
.
Thus we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{n}}(l_{n}(-),y)

\displaystyle\simeq\lim_{i:[k]\rightarrow[n]\in(\mathbb{G}/[n])^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Un}(\mathscr{P})}(-,i^{*}y)

\displaystyle\simeq\lim_{i:[k]\rightarrow[n]\in(\mathbb{G}/[n])^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(-,s(i^{*}y)).

Since
\mathscr{P}_{0}
admits all pullbacks, the latter presheaf is representable as desired.

Thus we conclude that
l
admits a fibered right adjoint over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
. Let us write

s^{\prime}:\mathrm{Un}(\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{0}

for this right adjoint followed by the projection
\mathscr{P}_{0}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{0}
. By the reasoning of above,
we see that
s^{\prime}
may be computed as

s^{\prime}(y)\simeq\lim_{i:[k]\rightarrow[n]\in(\mathbb{G}/[n])^{\mathrm{op}}}s(i^{*}y)

for
y\in\mathscr{P}_{n}
.
Hence, the universal property of spans proven by Haugseng in
[
Hau21b
, Corollary 3.10]
together with its succeeding remark,
asserts that
s^{\prime}
has a uniquely associated map

\rho:\mathrm{Un}(\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Un}(\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathscr{P}_{0}))

over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
that preserves cococartesian morphisms over inerts. Tracing through the proof of
[
Hau21b
, Corollary 3.10]
, we deduce the following:

(i)

The functor
\rho_{0}
is the identity.

(ii)

The functor
\rho_{1}
carries a horizontal arrow
F:x\rightarrow y
to the associated span
s(F)\rightarrow x\times y
.

(iii)

If
F:x\rightarrow y
and
G:y\rightarrow z
are
horizontal arrows, then the image of the cocartesian lift
(G,F)\rightarrow G\circ F
of
d_{1}:[2]\rightarrow[1]
in
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
factors in

\mathrm{Un}(\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathscr{P}_{0}))
as a composite

\rho(G,F)\rightarrow d_{1}^{*}(\rho(G,F))\rightarrow\rho(G\circ F)

where the first arrow is cocartesian. The second arrow lives in the fiber
\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathscr{P}_{0})_{1}
and is precisely computed as in (3).

Thus
\rho
straightens to the desired lax functor in light of the discussion in
[
Rui24
, Remark 5.24]
.
∎

Proof of
7.1
.

Since
\mathscr{P}
is tabular,
7.9
produces the candidate lax functor

\rho:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}).

which has the property that
\rho_{0}
recovers the identity.

Let us now assume that
\mathscr{P}
is an
\infty
-equipment. It remains to show that
\rho
is horizontally locally reflective if and only if conditions (1) and (2) are met.
Let
x,y
be objects of
\mathscr{P}
. Then we have to show that the functor

\rho_{x,y}:\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}/(x\times y)

has a right adjoint. I.e., we have to show that for every span
(p,q):e\rightarrow x\times y
the copresheaf

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}/(x\times y)}(e,\rho_{x,y}(-)):\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,y)\rightarrow\mathscr{S}

is corepresentable. If
F:x\rightarrow y
is a horizontal arrow in
\mathscr{P}
, then we have a natural pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}/(x\times y)}(e,\rho_{x,y}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(e,\rho_{x,y}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{(p,q)\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(e,x)\times\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(e,y).}

This can be rewritten as the natural pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}/(x\times y)}(e,\rho_{x,y}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{1}}(\mathrm{id}_{e},F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{(p,q)\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(e,x)\times\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(e,y)}

using the universal property of tabulating 2-cells.
Since
\mathscr{P}
admits all cocartesian 2-cells, we obtain that

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}/(x\times y)}(e,\rho_{x,y}F)\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,y)}({q}_{\circledast}{}{p}^{\circledast},F)

naturally in
F
. Thus
\rho_{x,y}
always has a right adjoint
L_{x,y}
that carries a span
(p,q):e\rightarrow x\times y
to the cocartesian lift
{q}_{\circledast}{}{p}^{\circledast}
. Now,
\rho_{x,y}
is fully faithful if and only if the counit

L_{x,y}\rho_{x,y}F\rightarrow F
is an equivalence for every horizontal arrow
F:x\rightarrow y
. But this is precisely the condition (1).
Moreover, using the description given in
7.9
of the lax compatibility 2-cells of
\rho
, it can be readily verified
that condition (2) is met if and only if
\rho
meets condition (2) of
4.4
.
∎

7.3.
Basic theory of fibrations

We will now fix a fibrational
\infty
-equipment
\mathscr{P}
and demonstrate that such an equipment supports a good theory of fibrations.

By
7.1
, there is a horizontally locally reflective span representation

\rho:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)})

so that we locally obtain an adjunction

L_{x,y}:\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}/(x\times y)\rightleftarrows\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,y):\rho_{x,y}

for objects
x
and
y
. The functor
\rho_{x,y}
is fully faithful in this case. The local objects of this adjunction are precisely
the two-sided discrete fibrations. The left adjoint
L_{x,y}
carries a span
(p,q):e\rightarrow x\times y

to the cocartesian lift
{q}_{\circledast}{}{p}^{\circledast}
.

Convention 7.10
.

We say that a two-sided discrete fibration
(p,q):e\rightarrow x\times y

classifies
a profunctor
F:x\rightarrow y

if
{q}_{\circledast}{}{p}^{\circledast}\simeq F,
i.e. when there exists a (necessarily cocartesian) tabulating 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

Proposition 7.11
.

Suppose that
e\rightarrow x\times y
is a two-sided discrete fibration that classifies a proarrow
F:x\rightarrow y
.
Given arrows
f:x^{\prime}\rightarrow x
and
g:y^{\prime}\rightarrow y
, we can form the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}\times y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\times y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times g}

and the left vertical arrow is again a two-sided discrete fibration that classifies the profunctor
{g}^{\circledast}{F}{f}_{\circledast}:x^{\prime}\rightarrow y^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The span representation
\rho
carries the cartesian 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g}^{\circledast}{F}{f}_{\circledast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

to a cartesian 2-cell in
\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)})
on account of
3.19
. The result follows directly from this observation.
∎

We have some suggestively named important examples of two-sided discrete fibrations:

Definition 7.12
.

Motivated by
6.14
, we will we write

(\mathrm{ev}_{1},\mathrm{ev}_{0}):a^{[1]}\rightarrow a^{\times 2}

for the two-sided discrete fibration that classifies the identity proarrow
\mathrm{id}_{a}
, with
a\in\mathscr{P}
. Moreover,
for arrows
f:x\rightarrow a
and
g:y\rightarrow a
, we define the
comma fibration

g\downarrow f\rightarrow x\times y

to be the two-sided discrete fibration that fits in the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\downarrow f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\times y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\times a,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{ev}_{1},\mathrm{ev}_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times g}

i.e. it is the one that classifies the proarrow
{g}^{\circledast}{}{f}_{\circledast}
.

We can easily obtain a fibrational variant of the Yoneda lemma, cf.
[
Str74
, Corollary 16]
and
[
RV22
, Theorem 5.7.1]
:

Proposition 7.13
.

Suppose that
f:x\rightarrow y
is an arrow in
\mathscr{P}
. Then the canonical map of spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{y}\downarrow f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\times y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{id},f)}

obtained by factoring the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f}_{\circledast}}

through the tabulating 2-cell of
{f}_{\circledast}
,
induces an equivalence on mapping spaces

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}/(x\times y)}(\mathrm{id}_{y}\downarrow f,e)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}/(x\times y)}(x,e)

for every two-sided discrete fibration
e\rightarrow x\times y
.

Proof.

This canonical map of spans is precisely the component of the unit
of the adjunction
(L_{x,y},\rho_{x,y})
at the span
(\mathrm{id},f):x\rightarrow x\times y
,
which is hence local with respect to the two-sided discrete fibrations.
∎

Definition 7.14
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is pointed, and let
x\in\mathscr{P}
. A two-sided discrete fibration from

x
to
\ast
is called a
left fibration
. Dually, a two-sided discrete fibration
from
\ast
to
x
is called a
right fibration
.

Proposition 7.15
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is cartesian closed and let
i\in\mathscr{P}
.
If
e\rightarrow x\times y
is a two-sided discrete fibration
classifying
F
, then
[i,e]\rightarrow[i,x]\times[i,y]
is the two-sided discrete fibration that
classifies
[i,F]
. In particular, we deduce that

[i,x^{[1]}]\simeq[i,x]^{[1]}.

Proof.

This follows directly from the fact that
[i,-]
is a normal lax right adjoint, and hence preserves
tabulating 2-cells.
∎

Using the comma fibrations, we can make sense of (co)slice objects in suitable equipments:

Definition 7.16
.

Suppose that we are in the case that
\mathscr{P}
is pointed and cartesian closed. Associated to an arrow
f:i\rightarrow x
in
\mathscr{P}
,
we can consider the following familiar commas:

(1)

the slice object

x/f:=\Delta\downarrow(\ast\xrightarrow{\{f\}}[i,x]),

(2)

the coslice object

f/x:=(\ast\xrightarrow{\{f\}}[i,x])\downarrow\Delta,

where
\Delta:x\rightarrow[i,x]
denotes the diagonal arrow. Both slice and coslice objects are canonically fibered over
x
as a right and left fibration respectively.

We will now demonstrate that particular weighted colimits (see
[
Rui23
, Subsection 6.2]
) can be computed as conical colimits (see
[
Rui23
, Definition 6.30]
). This recovers a
known fact that was proven in the case that
\mathscr{P}=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
by Haugseng
[
Hau22
, Theorem 1.2]
, cf. the discussion in
[
Rui23
, Example 6.14]
.

Proposition 7.17
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is pointed and cartesian closed. Let
f:i\rightarrow x
be an arrow in
\mathscr{P}
and

W:\ast\rightarrow i
be a weight corresponding to a right fibration

p:w\rightarrow i.
Then the proarrow
W
-cones under
f
exists
and it is classified by the left fibration
fp/x\rightarrow x
.

Proof.

In this case, the functor

\rho W\circ(-):\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}/x\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}/(x\times i)

admits a right adjoint
{}^{\rho W}{(-)}
since
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
is cartesian closed. This is explained in
[
Rui24
, Example 7.5]
.
The right adjoint carries a span
e\rightarrow x\times i
to the left arrow in the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[w,e]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[w,x]}\times{[w,i]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\times\{p\}}

in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}
where
\Delta:x\rightarrow[w,x]
denotes the diagonal.
In particular, it follows that if
e\rightarrow x\times i
is a two-sided discrete fibration classifying a proarrow
F
,
then
e^{\prime}\rightarrow x
is a left fibration classifying
{\{p\}}^{\circledast}{[w,F]}{\Delta}_{\circledast}
. It follows
from
4.9
that the functor

W\circ(-):\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,\ast)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,i)

has a right adjoint as well,
which is restricted from
{}^{\rho W}{(-)}
.
The proarrow of
W
-cones under
f
thus exists and is given by
{\{p\}}^{\circledast}{[w,f^{\circledast}]}{\Delta}_{\circledast}\simeq{\{p\}}^{\circledast}{[w,f]^{\circledast}}{\Delta}_{\circledast}\simeq{\{fp\}}^{\circledast}{}{\Delta}_{\circledast}
. This is precisely
the proarrow classified by
fp/x\rightarrow x
.
∎

Corollary 7.18
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is pointed and cartesian closed. Let
f:i\rightarrow x
be an arrow in
\mathscr{P}
and

W:\ast\rightarrow i
be a weight corresponding to a right fibration

p:w\rightarrow i.
Then the proarrow of
W
-cones under
f
coincides with the proarrow of conical cones under the composite
fp
, and both exist.

Corollary 7.19
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is pointed and cartesian closed.
Let
f:i\rightarrow x
and
w:i\rightarrow j
be arrows in
\mathscr{P}
. Suppose that
C
is the proarrow of
w^{\circledast}
-cones under

f
. Consider an element
\gamma:\ast\rightarrow j
of
j
, then
\gamma^{\circledast}C
is the proarrow of conical cones under the composite

i/\gamma\xrightarrow{p}i\xrightarrow{f}x,

where
p
denotes canonical right fibration

i/\gamma:=w\downarrow\gamma\rightarrow i,

or, equivalently, the one that sits in a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i/\gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j/\gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}

In particular, if the left Kan extension
g
of
f
along
w
exists, then it is given by

g(\gamma)=\mathop{colim}\displaylimits(i/\gamma\xrightarrow{p}i\xrightarrow{f}x).

Proof.

Note that
{\gamma^{\circledast}}{C}
is the proarrow of cones under
f
weighted by the proarrow
{w}^{\circledast}{}{\gamma}_{\circledast}
. This
can be deduced from the defining property of the proarrow of cones
C
and
3.17
.
Now the weight
{w}^{\circledast}{}{\gamma}_{\circledast}
is classified by the right fibration
p
so that

7.18
asserts that
\gamma^{\circledast}C
is the proarrow of conical cones under the composite
fp
.
∎

7.4.
Comprehensive factorizations

Recall that every functor between
\infty
-categories can be factored
into an initial functor followed by a left fibration.
2
2

2

See
[
Lur09
, Subsection 4.1.1]
for instance: in the model of quasi-categories, such a factorization can be obtained by factoring a functor into a left anodyne followed by a left fibration.
For ordinary categories,
this is called the
comprehensive factorization
of a functor
[
SW73
]
.
Since the classes of initial functors
and left fibrations are orthogonal to each other, these are part of an
orthogonal factorization system (see
[
Lur09
, Definition 5.2.8.8]
for a definition).

Now, for fibrational and pointed
\infty
-equipments, we also have a notion
of left fibrations and initial arrows (see
[
Rui23
, Definition 6.34]
). Hence,
we can pose the question: under what conditions
does such a comprehensive factorization system exist in these more general equipments? We first establish the following:

Proposition 7.20
.

Initial maps are left orthogonal to left fibrations
in a pointed and fibrational
\infty
-equipment.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a pointed and fibrational
\infty
-equipment.
Suppose that
f:i\rightarrow j
is an initial map, and that
p:e\rightarrow x
is a left fibration
in a pointed and fibrational
\infty
-equipment
\mathscr{P}
.
Then we have to show that the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}}(j,e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}}(i,e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}}(j,x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}}(i,x)}

of mapping spaces is a pullback square.
To this end, it suffices to check that for any map
q:j\rightarrow x
, the induced map

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}}(j,e)\times_{\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}}(j,x)}\{q\}\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}}(i,e)\times_{\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}}(i,x)}\{qf\}

is an equivalence.
But this is precisely the map

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}/x}(j,e)\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}/x}(i,e)

given by restriction along
f
that is now viewed as a map fibered over
x
.
Thus we have to show that
f
is local with respect to all left fibrations over
x
.
In other words, we have to check that the canonical 2-cell

{(tf)}_{\circledast}{}{(qf)}^{\circledast}\rightarrow{t}_{\circledast}{}{q}^{\circledast}

is an equivalence in
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,\ast)
, where
t
denotes the unique map
j\rightarrow\ast
.
This 2-cell is obtained by whiskering the canonical 2-cell
{(tf)}_{\circledast}{}{f}^{\circledast}\rightarrow{t}_{\circledast}
with

q^{\circledast}
, which is already an equivalence by the assumption that
f
is initial.
∎

Definition 7.21
.

We say that a pointed and fibrational
\infty
-equipment
\mathscr{P}
has
comprehensive factorizations

if the left class of initial arrows and right class of left fibrations
form an orthogonal factorization system on
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}
.

We can give a simpler description of those equipments that admit such factorizations:

Proposition 7.22
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a pointed and fibrational
\infty
-equipment, then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

\mathscr{P}
has comprehensive factorizations,

(2)

the class of left fibrations is closed under compositions and retracts.

Corollary 7.23
.

The
\infty
-equipment
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
admits comprehensive factorizations
for every
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
.

Proof.

Reasoning similarly as in the proof of
7.5
, we may use
the characterization of
7.22
to reduce to the case that
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{S}
, where it is already known to be true.
∎

Lemma 7.24
.

Suppose that
p:e\rightarrow x
is a left fibration in a pointed
and fibrational
\infty
-equipment
\mathscr{P}
with the property that for any left fibration
q:e^{\prime}\rightarrow e
, the composite

pq
is again a left fibration. Then the following holds true:

(1)

there exists a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(e,\ast)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,\ast)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}/e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}/x,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-){p}^{\circledast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{!}}

(2)

the functor

{(-)}{p}^{\circledast}:\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(e,\ast)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,\ast)

is conservative,

(3)

for any commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

of arrows in
\mathscr{P}
, the map
f
is initial if and only if
f
, viewed as a map in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}/x
, is an equivalence local to all the left fibrations over
x
.

Proof.

Throughout this proof, let us write
\rho:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)})
for the span representation of
\mathscr{P}
.
Note that
p_{!}
is conservative, so that (2) follows from (1). In turn, (3) follows from (2). To wit, the map

f
is initial if and only if the canonical 2-cell
{(tf)}_{\circledast}{}{f}^{\circledast}\rightarrow t_{\circledast}
is an equivalence, where

t:e\rightarrow\ast
denotes the terminal arrow. In light of (3), this is true if and only if the induced
2-cell
{(tf)}_{\circledast}{}{(pf)}^{\circledast}\rightarrow{t}_{\circledast}{}{p}^{\circledast}
is an equivalence. But this is precisely the condition for

f
to be a
\rho
-local 2-cell in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})/x
.

Let us prove (1).
A priori, the functor
\rho
only provides a
lax
commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(e,\ast)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,\ast)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}/e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}/x,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(-)}p^{\circledast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\downarrow\mathrm{id}_{x})^{*}}

where
p\downarrow\mathrm{id}_{x}=\rho(p^{\circledast})
. This means that for any proarrow
F:e\rightarrow\ast

we have a natural
\rho
-local 2-cell

\alpha_{F}:\rho F\circ(p\downarrow\mathrm{id}_{x})\rightarrow\rho({F}{p}^{\circledast})

in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}/x
. Recall that we have a canonical map

(p,\mathrm{id}_{e})\rightarrow(p\downarrow\mathrm{id}_{x}),

see also
7.13
. Precomposing with
\rho F
yields a comparison map

\beta_{F}:p_{!}\rho F=\rho F\circ(p,\mathrm{id}_{e})\rightarrow\rho F\circ(p\downarrow\mathrm{id}_{x})

that is natural in
F
. We claim that this map is a
\rho
-local 2-cell. Note that this
would finish the proof: then the composite
\beta_{F}\alpha_{F}:p_{!}\rho F\rightarrow\rho(Fp^{\circledast})

will be an equivalence since both domain (by assumption) and codomain are left fibrations.

Let us verify the remaining claim. One readily checks that
\beta_{F}
is precisely
the pushforward of the canonical
\rho
-local 2-cell

(p\circ\rho F,\mathrm{id}_{e^{\prime}})\rightarrow(p\circ\rho F)\downarrow\mathrm{id}_{x}

over
x\times e^{\prime}
along the projection
q:x\times e^{\prime}\rightarrow x
. Since
the right adjoint
q^{*}

preserves two-sided discrete fibrations, the left adjoint
q_{!}
preserves
\rho
-local equivalences
and hence
\beta_{F}
is a
\rho
-local equivalence.
∎

Proof of
7.22
.

It is clear that (2) implies (1), see also
[
Lur09
, Corollary 5.2.8.13]
. Conversely, suppose that (2) holds. It is readily
verified that initial arrows are closed under retracts in any pointed
\infty
-equipment. Hence, it remains to check
that every map
g:a\rightarrow x

can be factored into an initial arrow followed by a left fibration. Since
\mathscr{P}
is fibrational, we can find a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

of arrows in
\mathscr{P}
so that
p
is a left fibration and
f
is an equivalence local to all left fibrations over
x
. Hence
f
is initial by
7.24
and
we obtain the desired factorization
g\simeq pf
.
∎

These are direct consequences of
[
Lur09
, Proposition 5.2.8.11]
and
[
Lur09
, Proposition 5.8.6]
:

Corollary 7.25
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a pointed and fibrational
\infty
-equipment with comprehensive factorizations, then:

(1)

the class of left fibrations satisfy a cancellation property: if
p
is a left fibration, then
pf
is a left fibration if and only if
f
is a left fibration,

(2)

the class of initial arrows is closed under cobase change.

Proposition 7.26
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a fibrational and cartesian closed
\infty
-equipment with comprehensive factorizations. Let
\xi:\ast\rightarrow x

be an element of an object
x
of
\mathscr{P}
.
For a left fibration
p:e\rightarrow x
, the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

p
classifies the conjoint
{\xi}^{\circledast}:x\rightarrow\ast
,

(2)

there exists an equivalence
e\simeq\xi/x
above
x
,

(3)

\xi
factors as

\ast\rightarrow e\rightarrow x

so that the first map is initial.

Proof.

It is immediate that (1) and (2) are equivalent. Let us show that (2) and (3) are equivalent. As reasoned in
7.13
,
the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi/x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

is local with respect to left fibrations over
x
. Hence, (2) is equivalent to providing a triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

so that the top map is local with respect to left fibrations over
x
. In light of
7.24
,
this map is local if and only if it is initial.
∎

Corollary 7.27
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a fibrational and cartesian closed
\infty
-equipment with comprehensive factorizations. Let

\xi:\ast\rightarrow x
be an object of
x\in\mathscr{P}
. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

\xi
is the colimit of
f
,

(2)

there exists an equivalence
f/x\simeq\xi/x
between left fibrations above
x
,

(3)

\xi
factors as

\ast\rightarrow f/x\rightarrow x

so that the first arrow is initial.

Proof.

This follows from
7.26
and
7.17
.
∎

8.
Internal category theory via
\infty
-equipments

In this final section, we will fix an
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
and investigate the formal category theory
associated with the equipment

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})

that was constructed in
[
Rui23
]
. Note that this equipment has the following properties:

•

first of all, it is pointed,

•

it is cartesian closed (
5.11
),

•

it is horizontally closed (
4.12
),

•

it is fibrational (
7.5
),

•

it has comprehensive factorizations (
7.23
).

One may find a concise overview of the aspects of internal category theory that we will cover here in the introduction (
1.4
).
We stress again that most of these aspects have also been studied previously by Martini and Wolf in
[
Mar21
]
and
[
MW21
]
, but using a completely different approach.

Notation 8.1
.

For a category
\mathscr{C}
internal to
\mathscr{E}
, we will write
\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathscr{E}}(\mathscr{C},-)
for the normal lax right adjoint to
\mathscr{C}\times(-)
. In particular,
for another category
\mathscr{D}
internal to
\mathscr{E}
,
\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathscr{E}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
is the internal hom object in the
\infty
-category
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
. We will suppress the notation of
\mathscr{E}
if it is clear from the context.

Notation 8.2
.

For an object
E\in\mathscr{E}
, we will write
\pi_{E}:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}/E
for the étale geometric morphism. We have associated adjunctions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E,!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E,*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E}^{*}=E\times(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

where the top two functors are strict, and the bottom functor is normal and lax (see
3.11
).

We have the following push-pull formula on the level of equipments:

Proposition 8.3
.

For
E\in\mathscr{E}
, there exists a commutative diagram of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E)\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\times\pi_{E,!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E,*}\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\times-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E,!}(-\times-)}

In particular, for a category
\mathscr{C}
internal to
\mathscr{E}
, we have an equivalence

E\times\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathscr{E}}(\mathscr{C},-)\simeq\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathscr{E}/E}(E\times\mathscr{C},E\times(-))

between lax functors
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E)
.

Proof.

The first assertion follows from the fact that there exists similar commutative diagram of
\infty
-toposes before applying
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(-)
. This is a special case of the push-pull formula for étale morphisms
[
Lur09
, Remark 6.3.5.12]
.
The second assertion follows from unicity of right adjoints in the
(\infty,2)
-category
\mathrm{DBLCAT}^{\mathrm{lax}}_{\infty}
.
∎

8.1.
Generalized objects

As we have already observed in
[
Rui23
, Example 6.38]
, the
\infty
-equipment of internal categories
is virtually never strongly pointed. Whenever an equipment was strongly pointed, we had an efficient way of testing whether proarrows are companions or conjoints.
We commence our study of internal category theory by giving a generalization of this principle.

To this end, we note that we have an adjunction

\mathrm{incl}:\mathscr{E}\rightleftarrows\textup{Cat}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}):(-)^{\simeq}

induced by the adjunction of categories

\Delta\rightleftarrows\{[0]\}.

Note that this adjunction is natural in the
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
.
The right adjoint
(-)^{\simeq}
assigns to each internal category its
core
. The left adjoint
is fully faithful and the objects in the essential image of this inclusion are called
internal groupoids
. We will leave this inclusion
implicit from now on, and view every object of
\mathscr{E}
as an internal groupoid via this functor.

Remark 8.4
.

It can be verified that a category
\mathscr{C}
internal to
\mathscr{E}
is an internal groupoid if and only if it
is a groupoid representably (cf.
[
Str80
, Definition 1.7]
): i.e. for every category
X
internal to
\mathscr{E}
, the mapping
\infty
-category

\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})(X,\mathscr{C})

is a space.

Definition 8.5
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a category internal to
\mathscr{E}
. A
generalized object

of
\mathscr{C}
is a functor
x:E\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
so that
E\in\mathscr{E}
, i.e.
E
is an internal groupoid.
If
E=\ast
, then
x
is simply called an
object
of
\mathscr{C}
.

We say that a set
\mathcal{U}
of generalized objects of
\mathscr{C}

form a
groupoidal cover
of
\mathscr{C}
if the following map

\textstyle\coprodop\displaylimits_{E\rightarrow\mathscr{C}\in\mathcal{U}}E\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\simeq}

in
\mathscr{E}
, that is induced by the adjunct morphisms, is an effective epimorphism.

Remark 8.6
.

By adjunction, a generalized object
x:E=\pi_{E,!}(\ast)\rightarrow\mathscr{C}

of an internal category
\mathscr{C}
corresponds to an object of the category
E\times\mathscr{C}
internal to
\mathscr{E}/E
. We will (with abuse of notation) denote this latter adjunct object again by

x:E\rightarrow E\times\mathscr{C}.

Remark 8.7
.

Let
f:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}
be a geometric morphism. Then
the inverse image
f^{*}:\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
preserves effective epimorphisms.
Consequently, if
\mathcal{U}
is a groupoidal cover for
a category
\mathscr{C}
internal to
\mathscr{F}
, then
f^{*}\mathcal{U}:=\{f^{*}x\mid x\in\mathcal{U}\}
is again a groupoidal cover for
f^{*}\mathscr{C}
.

We can now formulate the following recognition theorem for companions and conjoints in the
\infty
-equipment
of categories internal to
\mathscr{E}
:

Theorem 8.8
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a groupoidal cover for a category
\mathscr{C}
internal to
\mathscr{E}
. Suppose
that
F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is a proarrow between internal categories. Then the following assertions are equivalent

(1)

F
is a companion,

(2)

for every generalized object
x:E\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
in
\mathcal{U}
, the restricted proarrow

{(E\times F)}{x_{\circledast}}:E\rightarrow E\times\mathscr{D}

in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})/E\simeq\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E)
is a companion.

Dually,
the following assertions are equivalent for a proarrow
G:\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
between internal categories:

(1)

G
is a conjoint,

(2)

for every generalized object
x:E\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
in
\mathcal{U}
, the restricted proarrow

x^{\circledast}{(E\times G)}:E\times\mathscr{D}\rightarrow E

in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})/E\simeq\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E)
is a conjoint.

To prove the above theorem, we will show that it holds for presheaf
\infty
-toposes via double categorical methods using
the theory developed in
[
Rui24
]
.
We then bootstrap this result to general
\mathscr{E}
. The bootstrapping
is achieved through three subsequent observations which we will present as lemmas:

Lemma 8.9
.

Suppose that
f:E\rightarrow X
is an effective epimorphism in
\mathscr{E}
. Then the pullback functor

f^{*}:\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/X)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E)

reflects companions and conjoints.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
X=\ast
, the terminal object of
\mathscr{E}
. By assumption, we have that
\ast
is given by the
Čech nerve of
f
,

\ast\simeq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}E^{\times(n+1)}.

Since
\mathscr{E}
is an
\infty
-topos, the functor
\mathscr{E}/(-):\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\widehat{\textup{Cat}}_{\infty}

preserves small limits. Thus we obtain an equivalence

\mathscr{E}\simeq\lim_{[n]\in\Delta}\mathscr{E}/E^{\times(n+1)}.

This gives rise to the following limit description

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\simeq\lim_{[n]\in\Delta}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E^{\times(n+1)})

on the level of
\infty
-equipments.
In light of
[
Rui23
, Hypothesis 3.10]
(cf.
[
Rui24
, Proposition 4.27]
), this entails that a proarrow
F
in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
is a companion (resp. conjoint)
if for any
n\geq 0
, the proarrow
E^{\times(n+1)}\times F
is a companion (resp. conjoint) in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E^{\times(n+1)})
. This, in turn, can be reduced to
n=1
, since the terminal map

E^{\times(n+1)}\rightarrow\ast
factors over
E\rightarrow\ast
via one of the projections.
∎

Lemma 8.10
.

Suppose that
F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is a proarrow between internal categories. If
there exists a groupoidal cover
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathscr{C}

so that condition (2) of
8.8
is met, then this condition holds with
respect to any groupoidal cover
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

We just handle the case for companions since the dual case for conjoints is similar.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
both consist
of just one generalized object
x:E\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
and
y:E^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
respectively.
We can now define an internal groupoid
E^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{E}
by the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}

Since effective epimorphisms are closed under base change,

p
and
q
are effective epimorphisms. Now, one can readily compute that

p^{*}({(E\times F)}x_{\circledast})\simeq{(E^{\prime\prime}\times F)}(xp)_{\circledast}\simeq q^{*}((E^{\prime}\times F)y_{\circledast})

Thus the result follows from
8.9
.
∎

Lemma 8.11
.

Suppose that
i:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}
is a geometric embedding between
\infty
-toposes.
If
8.8
holds for
\mathscr{F}
, then
it holds for
\mathscr{E}
as well.

Proof.

We just handle the case for companions since the dual case for conjoints is similar.
Suppose that
F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is a proarrow between categories internal to
\mathscr{E}
so that
condition (2) of
8.8
is met. In light of
8.10
, this condition
does not depend on the chosen cover
\mathcal{U}
. In particular, we may assume that
\mathcal{U}=\{x:\mathscr{C}^{\simeq}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}\}
.
We must check that
F
is a companion. Since
F\simeq i^{*}i_{*}F
, it will be necessary and sufficient to check that

i_{*}F
is a companion in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{F})
.

Note that
i
induces
a geometric morphism on slices that fits in a commutative diagram of
geometric morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}/\mathscr{C}^{\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{F}/i_{*}\mathscr{C}^{\simeq}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i/\mathscr{C}^{\simeq}}

If we apply the normal lax functor
(i/\mathscr{C}^{\simeq})_{*}
to the companion proarrow

{(\mathscr{C}^{\simeq}\times F)}x_{\circledast}
,
then
3.18
asserts that we recover

{(i_{*}\mathscr{C}^{\simeq}\times i_{*}F)}{(i_{*}x)_{\circledast}}:i_{*}\mathscr{C}^{\simeq}\rightarrow i_{*}\mathscr{C}^{\simeq}\times i_{*}\mathscr{D}.

Thus this proarrow is again a companion by
3.20
. Since
i_{*}x
is precisely the canonical cover
(i_{*}\mathscr{C})^{\simeq}\rightarrow i_{*}\mathscr{C}
, the assumption on
\mathscr{F}
implies
that
i_{*}F
is a companion.
∎

Proof of
8.8
.

On account of
8.11
, we may reduce to the case that
\mathscr{E}=\textup{PSh}(T)
for some small
\infty
-category
T
.
Let us show the only non-trivial assertion that (2) implies (1) for the case of companions.
Note that we can identify

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
with the cotensor
[T^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}]
. In light of
[
Rui24
, Theorem 4.6]
, we have to check
that for each arrow
\phi:s\rightarrow t
of
T
, the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}(t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{s}}

in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
is
companionable
. That entails checking the following conditions:

(i)

the proarrow
F_{t}
is the companion of a functor
f_{t}:\mathscr{C}(t)\rightarrow\mathscr{D}(t)
for every
t\in T
,

(ii)

and moreover, the induced natural transformation
\phi^{*}f_{t}\rightarrow f_{s}\phi^{*}
by the pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}(t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}(s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}(s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{s}}

is an equivalence. Here the top and bottom 2-cell are the companionship unit and counit respectively.

By the Yoneda lemma, any object
x\in\mathscr{C}(t)
corresponds to a map

y_{t}:=\mathrm{Map}_{T}(-,t)\rightarrow\mathscr{C},

which we will again denote by
x
. This gives rise to a groupoidal cover

\{x:y_{t}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}\mid t\in T,x\in\mathscr{C}(t)\}

of
\mathscr{C}
.
By what we have proven in
8.10
, we may assume that this is the cover
\mathcal{U}
. Let
x:y_{t}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}\in\mathcal{U}
. Note that the
étale morphism
\pi_{y_{t}}:\textup{PSh}(T)\rightarrow\textup{PSh}(T/t)
is induced by the projection
\pi_{t}:(T/t)^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow T^{\mathrm{op}}
. Consequently, under the identifications of
2.25
,
the functor
\pi_{y_{t}}^{*}=y_{t}\times(-)
is given by the functor

\pi_{t}^{*}:[T^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}]\rightarrow[(T/t)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}].

So, by assumption
{\pi_{t}^{*}F}{x_{\circledast}}
is a companion in
[(T/t)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}]
, where we now view
x
as a functor
\ast\rightarrow\pi_{t}^{*}\mathscr{C}
indexed by
T/t
(cf.
8.6
).
Consequently, for any arrow
\phi:s\rightarrow t
, the composite 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}(t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{\circledast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi^{*}x)_{\circledast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{s}}

is companionable in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
.

We can now conclude the proof as follows. In particular, the above implies that
(F_{t})x_{\circledast}
is a companion for every
t\in T
and
x\in\mathscr{C}(t)
, so that (i) holds
by the Yoneda lemma, see
[
Rui23
, Example 6.38]
. Moreover, from the companionability of the above composite 2-cells, it can be deduced that the component

\phi^{*}f_{t}(x)\rightarrow f_{s}\phi^{*}(x)

of the natural transformation in (ii) at
x\in\mathscr{C}(t)

is an equivalence.
∎

8.2.
Internal fibrations and colimits

Internal
\infty
-categories
support a notion of two-sided discrete, left and right fibrations on account of
7
. In this instance, this a representable notion:

Proposition 8.12
.

Let
(p,q):A\rightarrow\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{D}
be a span of categories internal to
\mathscr{E}
. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

the span
(p,q)
is a two-sided discrete fibration of categories internal to
\mathscr{E}
,

(2)

for every category
X
internal to
\mathscr{E}
, the span

(p,q)_{*}:\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})(X,A)\rightarrow\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(X,\mathscr{C})\times\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(X,\mathscr{D})

is a two-sided discrete fibration of
\infty
-categories,

(3)

condition (2) holds for all groupoids
X\in\mathscr{E}
.

Proof.

It directly follows from
6.22
that (1) implies (2). It is also clear that (2) implies (3). Thus it remains to show that (3) implies (1). To this end,
we first observe the following. Suppose that
i:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}
is a geometric embedding.
Then for every
Y\in\mathscr{F}
, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})(i^{*}Y,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})(i^{*}Y,\mathscr{C})\times\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})(i^{*}Y,\mathscr{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(\mathscr{F})(Y,i_{*}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(\mathscr{F})(Y,i_{*}\mathscr{C})\times\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(\mathscr{F})(Y,i_{*}\mathscr{D}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p,q)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{*}p,i_{*}q)_{*}}

Thus if (3) holds for
(p,q)
, then (3) holds for
(i_{*}p,i_{*}q)
as well. Taking
Y=i_{*}X
with
X\in\mathscr{E}
, we see that the converse implication is true as well.
Moreover, since
i_{*}
reflects tabulations (see
), (1) holds for
(p,q)
if and only if it holds for
(i_{*}p,i_{*}q)
.

Now, it follows from the above that it is necessary and sufficient to show that (3) implies (1) in the prototypical case that
\mathscr{E}=\textup{PSh}(T)
for some small
\infty
-category
T
. Then

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\simeq[T^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}]
. We have to check that the cocartesian 2-cell
\mathrm{id}_{A}\rightarrow{q}_{\circledast}{}{p}^{\circledast}
is tabulating
and this can be checked point-wise on account of
. Consequently, we deduce that
(p,q)
is a two-sided discrete fibration if and only if

(p(t),q(t)):A(t)\rightarrow\mathscr{C}(t)\times\mathscr{D}(t)
is a two-sided discrete fibration. But this can be identified with the post-composition functor
(p,q)_{*}
at the internal groupoid
X:=\mathrm{Map}_{T}(-,t)
.
∎

We also have the following generalization of
[
Cis19
, Proposition 4.4.11]
, see also
[
Mar21
, Proposition 4.4.7]
:

Proposition 8.13
.

Left fibrations are proper and right fibrations are smooth in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
.

Proof.

We will just handle the case of left fibrations; the other case is similar.
Let
f:X\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
be a left fibration in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
. Since left fibrations are closed under pullbacks, it is necessary and sufficient
to check that every pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}

of categories internal to
\mathscr{E}
is exact. We may choose a presentation
i:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\textup{PSh}(T)
so that
4.3
asserts that it suffices to check that
the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}\mathscr{C}}

is exact in
[T^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}]
. But this may be checked level-wise at objects
t\in T
on account of
2.26
, see also the argument in

4.29
. Thus
the desired result follows
from the fact that left fibrations of
\infty
-categories are proper, see
[
Cis19
, Proposition 4.4.11]
or
[
Lur09
, Proposition 4.1.2.15]
.
∎

Note that for an internal category, the comma two-sided discrete
fibration
\mathscr{C}^{[1]}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\times 2}
of
7.12
is computed as

(\mathrm{ev}_{1},\mathrm{ev}_{0}):\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\times 2}.

Consequently, the coslice
f/\mathscr{C}
associated with a functor
f:I\rightarrow\mathscr{C}

sits in a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f/\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1]\times I,\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C})\times\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{ev}_{1},\mathrm{ev}_{0})}

and the left arrow is the associated left fibration. We can now interpret
7.27
:

Corollary 8.14
.

Let
f:I\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
be an internal functor. Suppose that
W:\ast\rightarrow I
is a weight classified by a
right fibration
p:E\rightarrow I
. Let
x
be an object of
\mathscr{C}
. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

x
is the
W
-weighted colimit of
f
,

(2)

there exists an equivalence
fp/\mathscr{C}\simeq x/\mathscr{C}
over
\mathscr{C}
,

(3)

there exists an initial object of
fp/\mathscr{C}
that lies over
x
.

In case that
W
corresponds to the coconical weight
[
Rui23
, Definition 6.30]
classified by
p=\mathrm{id}_{I}
, this was used as the definition of (conical)
colimits by Martini and Wolf
[
MW21
]
. Consequently, we obtain:

Corollary 8.15
.

The notion of colimits in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
coincides with the notion developed by Martini and Wolf.

We may also characterize initial and final functors using an internal version of Quillen’s theorem A (cf.
[
Rui23
, Proposition 6.39]
). This result was obtained by Martini as well
[
Mar21
, Corollary 4.4.8]
, using a different method.
To formulate this, we need the fact that the inclusion

\mathrm{incl}:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})

admits a left adjoint as well that is natural in the
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
. The left adjoint is described by

(-)[(-)^{-1}]:\textup{Cat}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathscr{E}:\mathscr{C}\mapsto\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathscr{C}_{n},

and was discussed in the text following Corollary 3.2.12 of
[
Mar21
]
.
The image
\mathscr{C}[\mathscr{C}^{-1}]
of
\mathscr{C}\in\textup{Cat}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
is usually called the
classifying groupoid
of
\mathscr{C}
.

This adjunction can also be described as follows.
Note that by
8.4
and
8.12
, the reflective inclusion

\rho_{\ast,\ast}:\mathrm{Hor}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}))(\ast,\ast)\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})

provided by the span representation
is an equivalence onto the internal groupoids. Hence, we obtain a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}))(\ast,\ast)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Cat}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\ast,\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{incl}}

Recall that
\rho_{\ast,\ast}
has a left adjoint
L_{\ast,\ast}

with a concrete description (see
7.1
) that allows us to conclude that the terminal
map
\mathscr{C}[\mathscr{C}^{-1}]\rightarrow\ast
is precisely the two-sided discrete fibration classifying
{t}_{\circledast}{}{t}^{\circledast}=L_{\ast,\ast}(t)

where

t:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\ast
denotes the terminal arrow.

Proposition 8.16

(Quillen’s theorem A internally)
.

Let
J
be an internal category with groupoidal cover
\mathcal{U}
.
Then a functor
f:I\rightarrow J
between internal categories is final if and only if for every generalized
object
x:E\rightarrow J
in
\mathcal{U}
, we have

x/(E\times I)[(x/(E\times I))^{-1}]\simeq E

in
\mathscr{E}/E
.

Proof.

By definition (see
[
Rui23
, Definition 6.34]
),
f
is final if and only if the proarrow
{f}_{\circledast}{}{t}^{\circledast}:\ast\rightarrow J
is a conjoint where

t:I\rightarrow\ast
denotes the terminal arrow.
On account of
8.8
, this is equivalent to asking that the proarrow

G:=x^{\circledast}{(E\times{f}_{\circledast}{}{t}^{\circledast})}\simeq x^{\circledast}(E\times f)_{\circledast}(E\times t)^{\circledast}:E\rightarrow E

is a conjoint in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E)
or every
x:E\rightarrow J
in
\mathcal{U}
.
Note that
{x}^{\circledast}{}{(E\times f)}_{\circledast}
is classified by the left fibration
x/(E\times I)\rightarrow E\times I
internal to
\mathscr{E}/E
.
In the proof of
7.24
, we have shown that there is a map from the terminal arrow

\tau:x/(E\times I)\rightarrow E\times I\xrightarrow{E\times t}E

to the left fibration that classifies
G
, which is local with respect to all left fibrations.
Hence, we deduce from the discussion preceding the proposition that
G
is classified by the classifying
groupoid of
x/(E\times I)\in\textup{Cat}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E)
. It is a conjoint precisely if it is the conjoint of the unique functor
E\rightarrow E
, which is classified by
E
.
∎

8.3.
Internal Kan extensions

As a concluding application of the developed theory of equipments, we will study point-wise Kan extensions
[
Rui23
, Section 6.3]

internal to
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
. Using our methods, we are able to give a quick proof of the following result for the existence and computation of left Kan extensions internal to
\mathscr{E}
, which was
earlier established by Shah
[
Sha18
]
in the case that
\mathscr{E}
is a presheaf topos:

Proposition 8.17
.

Let
f:I\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
and
w:I\rightarrow J
be internal functors.
Suppose that
\mathcal{U}
is a groupoidal cover for
J
.
Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

the left Kan extension of
f
along
w
exists,

(2)

for each generalized element
x:E\rightarrow J
in
\mathcal{U}
,
the colimit of the functor

(E\times I)/x:=(E\times J)/x\times_{E\times J}(E\times I)\rightarrow E\times I\xrightarrow{E\times f}E\times\mathscr{C}

internal to
\mathscr{E}/E
exists.

In this case, the left Kan extension
g
is given on
a generalized object
x:E\rightarrow J
by
the colimit

(E\times g)(x)=\mathop{colim}\displaylimits((E\times I)/x\rightarrow E\times I\rightarrow E\times\mathscr{C})

internal to
\mathscr{E}/E
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
is horizontally closed (see
4.12
), there exists a proarrow
C:J\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
of
w^{\circledast}
-cones under
f
.
Let
x:E\rightarrow J
be a generalized object. Then the functor
E\times(-)=\pi_{E}^{\ast}
is proper, see
8.13
.
Consequently,
E\times C
is a proarrow of
(E\times w)^{\circledast}
-cones under
E\times f
. Hence, it follows from

7.19
that
x^{\circledast}{(E\times C)}
is the proarrow of conical cones under the composite
(E\times I)/x\rightarrow E\times I\rightarrow E\times\mathscr{C}
.
The desired conclusions can now be deduced from
8.8
.
∎

The above formula for the left Kan extension also appears in
[
MW21
, Remark 6.3.6]
. Our next goal
is to study the internal functors of Kan extensions using the analysis of
5.2
. We will see later that the following equivalent conditions are sufficient for the existence of these:

Lemma 8.18
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a category and
w:I\rightarrow J
be a functor internal to
\mathscr{E}
.
Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

There exists a groupoidal cover
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C})
so that for every
f:E\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C})\in\mathcal{U}
,
the left Kan extension of
f
, viewed as a functor
E\times I\rightarrow E\times\mathscr{C}
, along
E\times w
internal to
\mathscr{E}/E
exists.

(2)

There exists a groupoidal cover
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C})
so that for every
f:E\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C})\in\mathcal{U}
,
there exists a groupoidal cover
\mathcal{V}_{f}
of the category
E\times J
internal to
\mathscr{E}/E
with the property that
the colimit

(E^{\prime}\times E\times I)/x\rightarrow E^{\prime}\times E\times I\xrightarrow{E^{\prime}\times f}E^{\prime}\times E\times\mathscr{C}

internal to
\mathscr{E}/(E^{\prime}\times E)
exists for any
x:E^{\prime}\rightarrow E\times J
in
\mathcal{V}_{f}
.

(3)

Assertion (1) holds for any choice of groupoidal cover
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C})
.

(4)

Assertion (2) holds for any choice of groupoidal cover
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C})
and family of groupoidal covers
\{\mathcal{V}_{f}\}_{f\in\mathcal{U}}
.

Proof.

Note that the assertions (1) and (2), and the assertions (3) and (4) are equivalent on account of
8.17
. Clearly, (3) implies (1) and
hence it remains to show that (1) implies (3). So, assume that (1) holds. Let
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
be another
cover of
\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C})
. We may assume that
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
consist of one generalized element
f:E\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C})

and
g:E^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C})
respectively. Consider the internal groupoid
E^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{E}
with the defining feature that it sits in a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Let
C
be the proarrow of
(E^{\prime}\times w)^{\circledast}
-cones under
g:E^{\prime}\times I\rightarrow E^{\prime}\times\mathscr{C}
in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E^{\prime})
. As

q
is an effective epimorphism,
C
will be a conjoint if

q^{*}C
is a conjoint in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E^{\prime\prime})
(see
8.9
). But
q
is proper (see
8.13
)
and this means that
q^{*}C
is the proarrow
of
(E^{\prime\prime}\times w)^{\circledast}
-cones under
q^{*}g\simeq p^{*}f
on account of
4.16
. By the same argument,
q^{*}C
is the image of the proarrow of
(E\times w)^{\circledast}
-cones
under
f:E\times I\rightarrow E\times\mathscr{C}
in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E)
, which was a conjoint by assumption.
∎

Next, we will now show the existence of functors of Kan extensions under these conditions. This was
shown before by Shah
[
Sha18
, Theorem 10.5]
in case that
\mathscr{E}
is a presheaf topos, and by Martini and Wolf
[
MW21
, Theorem 6.3.5]
in the general case.

Proposition 8.19
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a category and
w:I\rightarrow J
be a functor internal to
\mathscr{E}
.
If the equivalent conditions of
8.18
are met,
then the internal functor

w^{*}:\mathrm{Fun}(J,\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C})

admits a left adjoint

w_{!}:\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(J,\mathscr{C}).

The value of the left adjoint on generalized elements of
\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C})
can be described as follows. For every groupoid
E\in\mathscr{E}
,
the functor

E\times w_{!}:\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathscr{E}/E}(E\times I,E\times\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathscr{E}/E}(E\times J,E\times\mathscr{C})

internal to
\mathscr{E}/E
carries
a functor
f:E\times I\rightarrow E\times\mathscr{C}
to its left Kan extension along
E\times w
internal to
\mathscr{E}/E
.

Proof.

The functor
w^{*}
admits a left adjoint if the proarrow

w^{*}_{\circledast}:\mathrm{Fun}(J,\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C})

is a conjoint in light of
[
Rui23
, Corollary 6.6]
. In light of
8.8
, it suffices to check that for any
f:E\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathscr{E}}(I,\mathscr{C})
, the proarrow

f^{\circledast}(E\times w)^{*}_{\circledast}:\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathscr{E}/E}(E\times J,E\times\mathscr{C})\rightarrow E

is a conjoint in
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E)
. Here we used that the arrow
E\times w^{*}
can be identified
with
(E\times w)^{\ast}
under the push-pull identifications of
8.3
. The desired result follows now from
5.13
.
∎

Remark 8.20
.

In the context of
8.18
, note that assertion (2) is met whenever there exists a groupoidal cover
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathscr{C}
with the following property:
for every
x:E\rightarrow J\in\mathcal{U}
,
E\times\mathscr{C}
admits those colimits internal to
\mathscr{E}/E
that are shaped by the internal category
(E\times I)/x
. This condition does not depend on
\mathcal{U}
. This is essentially
the assumption that the authors of
[
Sha18
]
and
[
MW21
]
put on
\mathscr{C}
in their respective versions of
8.17
.

Recall that we have a notion of fully faithful functors internal to
\mathscr{E}
, by interpreting the abstract notion defined in
[
Rui23
, Definition 6.1]
in the equipment
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
.
This gives the following expected result:

Proposition 8.21
.

In the situation of
8.19
, the resulting functor
w_{!}
will be fully faithful if
w
is fully faithful.

To this end, we will need some analysis of the internal fully faithful functors. In particular, we will show that it coincides Martini’s notion
[
Mar21
, Definition 3.8.1]
.

Proposition 8.22
.

Let
f:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
be a functor internal to
\mathscr{E}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

the functor
f
is fully faithful,

(2)

the functor
f
is representably fully faithful, i.e. for every category
X
internal to
\mathscr{E}
,
the functor

f_{*}:\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})(X,\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})(X,\mathscr{D})

between
\infty
-categories is fully faithful,

(3)

condition (2) holds for all groupoids
X\in\mathscr{E}
.

Proof.

Note that (1) implies (2) on account of
[
Rui23
, Proposition 6.4]
. It is clear that (2) implies (3).
To show that (3) implies (1), we note that reflective inclusions of double
\infty
-categories
reflect cartesian cells, and hence fully faithfulness. Thus a similar argument as
8.12
applies
so that may reduce to the
prototypical case that
\mathscr{E}=\textup{PSh}(T)
for some small
\infty
-category
T
. Then

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\simeq[T^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}]
. Since cartesian cells
are detected point-wise in this cotensor (see
2.26
),
f
is fully faithful if and only if
f(t):\mathscr{C}(t)\rightarrow\mathscr{D}(t)
is fully faithful. But this can be identified with the post-composition functor
f_{*}
at the internal groupoid
X=\mathrm{Map}_{T}(-,t)
,
which is fully faithful by assumption.
∎

Corollary 8.23
.

The notion of internal fully faithful functors coincides with that of Martini
[
Mar21
]
.

Proof.

This follows because characterization (3) of
8.22
is precisely characterization (3) of
[
Mar21
, Proposition 3.8.7]
.
∎

Proof.

On account of
8.22
,
w_{!}
is fully faithful if and only if for every
E\in\mathscr{E}
, the induced functor

(w_{!})_{*}:\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})(E,\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C}))\rightarrow\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})(E,\mathrm{Fun}(J,\mathscr{C}))

is fully faithful. On account of
5.12
, this is left adjoint to the (external) restriction functor
(E\times w)^{*}:\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})(E\times J,\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})(E\times I,\mathscr{C})
,
which, by adjunction, is given by

(E\times w)^{*}:\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E)(E\times J,E\times\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\textup{CAT}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}/E)(E\times I,E\times\mathscr{C}).

The result now follows from Corollary 6.21 and Proposition 6.22 of
[
Rui23
]
, because the functor
E\times(-)
preserves fully faithfulness, hence
the functor
E\times w
is again fully faithful internal to
\mathscr{E}/E
.
∎

*
